The third is the most concrete, and it fits the narrative exactly. The youths were about to leave their city, their families, and every social tie they had. Guidance in that moment was not additional information about God; it was the capacity to act on what they already knew.

Al-Sulamī's reading emphasises understanding and discernment, while al-Ṭabarī stresses patience. The two are not rivals. Discernment tells a person what must be done; patience makes it possible to do it.

Notice what the increase does not include. The youths are not given information about their future, the length of their sleep, or the outcome of their flight. The guidance granted is sufficient for the next decision and no further, which is how the Qur'an consistently describes divine help to people in crisis.

**The Same Increase in Sūrat al-Fatḥ**

The parallel is worth holding beside this verse: *“He is the One Who sent down serenity upon the hearts of the believers so that they may increase even more in their faith. To Allah ˹alone˺ belong the forces of the heavens and the earth”* (48:4).

Both passages describe faith increasing under pressure, and both attribute the increase to a divine act rather than to human resolve. The cave youths received theirs before standing up to a king; the companions received theirs before facing an army. The mechanism is the same, and in both cases it is described as something done to the heart rather than something the heart manages on its own.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:14

> And We strengthened their hearts when they stood up and declared, “Our Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth. We will never call upon any god besides Him, or we would truly be uttering an outrageous lie.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Hearts Tied Fast Before Any Word Was Spoken**

The verse opens with an act performed on the youths rather than by them: We bound their hearts. The verb *rabaṭa* describes tying something firmly — a rope made fast, a load secured — and applied to the heart it means that the interior was held together under pressure.

The sequence is the point. Strengthening comes first, declaration second. What the youths said in public was made possible by something done in them beforehand, and the verse declines to present their courage as self-generated.

The same verb appears in a military context at Badr: *“And He sent down rain from the sky to purify you, free you from Satan’s whispers, strengthen your hearts, and make ˹your˺ steps firm”* (8:11). In both places the strengthening is paired with a situation in which fear would be the reasonable response, and in both it is described as a gift.

**Standing Up: Two Readings of the Moment**

*Idh qāmū*, when they stood, admits two readings, and the commentators divide.

The first takes it literally and publicly: the youths rose before the tyrant king and refused his demand that they worship his gods. On this reading the declaration is an act of defiance delivered to the person with power to punish it. The second reads the standing as the moment when the group, having each harboured his belief secretly, met by chance outside the city, the eldest confessed his monotheism, and the others found themselves in agreement.

The grounds differ in kind. The first fits the plain sense of a public declaration addressed to rulers; the second explains how a group of unrelated young men came to act together. The second reading has the advantage of making the following verse intelligible, since the youths there speak to one another rather than to a king. Both can be held together: agreement reached privately, then declared publicly.

**An Argument Drawn From Creation**

The declaration's first sentence is not a claim of loyalty but a statement of scope: *Our Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth*. The youths do not begin with what they were taught or with whom they belong to. They begin with what God rules.

The move is argumentative. A deity limited to a city, a tribe, or a temple can be one option among others. A Lord whose domain is the heavens and the earth cannot be supplemented, because there is nothing outside his domain for another god to govern. The second half of their sentence — we will never call on any god besides him — follows from the first as a conclusion rather than an addition.

This is the same argument Abraham makes in the Qur'an, and the youths are not reported to have learned it from anyone. They state it as something they saw.

The argument also disposes of the local gods without naming them. No idol needs to be described or refuted once the scope of the true Lord has been stated, because an idol is by definition inside that scope and therefore inside his jurisdiction rather than beside it.

**The Alternative Named as a Lie**

The conditional clause is unusually strong. They do not say that calling on another god would be wrong, or mistaken, or forbidden. They say it would be *shataṭan* — an outrageous claim, a statement that has travelled far past the bounds of what can be said.

The word denotes excess and distance from the middle. Applied to speech, it marks a claim so remote from reality that it is not a competing opinion but a fabrication. The youths therefore do not treat idolatry as an error they might have held; they treat it as a falsehood they would be uttering.

Two consequences follow. The refusal is not a preference that could be reversed by better terms, since a lie does not become acceptable when offered concessions. And the youths locate the wrong precisely: the offence lies in what would be *said*, in speech that misdescribes God.

**Courage Granted, Not Mustered**

Read together, the two halves of the verse describe a single mechanism. God binds the heart; the heart speaks. Neither half is omitted, and neither is made to do the other's work.

The theological stakes are real. If the declaration had been presented as the product of the youths' temperament, the story would be about brave young men, and its value would be admiration. Presented as a strengthened heart speaking, it becomes a description of how any believer stands under pressure — by something given rather than by something summoned.

The verse does not reduce the youths to instruments. They are the ones who stand and the ones who speak, and the words are theirs. What is denied is that the capacity came from them alone.

The practical consequence is a discipline rather than a doctrine. Since the strengthening is asked for rather than manufactured, the believer's task before a confrontation is prayer, and the confrontation itself is where the answer becomes visible.

**The Same Declaration From Abraham**

The parallel is close enough to be exact: *“He replied, ‘In fact, your Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth, Who created them ˹both˺. And to that I bear witness.’”* (21:56). A young man, addressing his people's religion, grounds his refusal in the scope of God's creation and adds a personal testimony.

The Qur'an sets the two statements side by side across different sūrahs without comment, and the effect is to place the cave youths inside a lineage. They are not an isolated case of youthful courage but one instance of a declaration that prophets before them had already made — and made, in Abraham's case, in the same sentence about the Lord of the heavens and the earth (6:78).

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:15

> ˹Then they said to one another,˺ “These people of ours have taken gods besides Him. Why do they not produce a clear proof of them? Who then does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Demand for Evidence, Not for Devotion**

The youths do not argue that their people's gods are ugly, cruel, or unworthy. They ask for a proof. The noun is *sulṭān*, an authority or warrant — the thing that would entitle a claim to be believed — qualified by *mubīn*, clear and manifest.

The demand is unusual in a religious dispute. Most arguments between rival cults in the ancient world were conducted through appeal to antiquity, to local custom, or to the visible success of a temple. The youths bypass all of that and ask a single question: on what evidence does this worship rest?

The commentators draw a general principle from the question. Worship must be grounded in intellectual proof and cannot rest on imitation alone. On this reading the verse is not only a moment in a story but a statement about what makes a religious claim admissible.

The demand is also reciprocal in effect. A person who asks for proof of another's gods is implicitly agreeing to be asked for proof of his own. The youths are prepared for that, since their own claim rests on an argument they can state in a sentence.

**Imitation as What the Demand Excludes**

*Taqlīd* — following another without knowing his grounds — is the practice the question is aimed at, and the Qur'an attacks it repeatedly. When people are told to follow what God has revealed, they answer: *“No! We ˹only˺ follow what we found our forefathers practicing”* (2:170), and the reply asks whether the forefathers understood anything at all.

The connection to the cave youths is direct. Their own people's worship had exactly that structure: inherited, unexamined, defended by its age. The youths' question removes the only defence available to it, because antiquity is not a proof.

There is a second edge. The question is addressed to the youths' own community — *these people of ours*. They are not interrogating strangers. They are asking their own people for grounds that their people have never been asked to give.

That makes the question harder to dismiss. An outsider's demand for proof can be answered by appeal to belonging; a demand from inside the community removes even that refuge.

**The Rhetorical Question That Settles It**

The final clause is not really a question. *Who does more wrong than the one who fabricates a lie against God?* expects no answer because none exists; it states a maximum.

The formula recurs across the Qur'an with almost identical wording: *“Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or deny His signs?”* (6:21); *“Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or deny His revelations?”* (10:17); *“And who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or reject the truth after it has reached them?”* (29:68); *“Who does more wrong than the one who fabricates lies about Allah when invited to submit ˹to Him˺?”* (61:7).

The repetition marks a category. Fabrication against God is treated as the ceiling of wrongdoing, above murder, above oppression, above denial. The reason is structural: every other wrong distorts something created, while this one distorts the description of the Creator on which every other judgement depends.

**Fabrication as the Specific Wrong**

The word used is *iftarā*, to invent or forge. It is not the word for error, ignorance, or misjudgement, and the difference is the point.

An error is made by someone reaching for the truth and missing. Fabrication is making something up and presenting it as divine. The Qur'an reserves this verb for religious invention specifically, and it never softens it.

Applied to the youths' people, the charge is precise. They have taken gods besides God, and they cannot produce a warrant; the absence of a warrant, combined with the confidence of the claim, converts the practice into invention. The youths are not accusing them of confusion. They are accusing them of authorship.

The accusation is heavier than it sounds in a tribal setting. To invent a god is to authorise a social order — sacrifices, festivals, obligations, hierarchies — in a name that has no warrant behind it. The wrong is not confined to belief; it extends to everything built on the belief.

**Three Moves in a Single Sentence**

The argument is compact enough to be set out formally, and its compactness is part of its force.

First, a fact: these people of ours have taken gods besides Him. No one disputes it, including them. Second, a demand: let them produce a clear proof of these gods. The demand is open to anyone and excludes no kind of evidence. Third, a conclusion drawn from the demand's failure: whoever fabricates lies against God does the greatest wrong.

The middle step does all the work. Because no proof can be produced — and the verse assumes that none can — the practice is left with no support except the will of those performing it. What remains is invention, and invention attributed to God is the wrong the final clause names.

The structure explains why the Qur'an returns to this argument so often. It is short, it is available to anyone regardless of learning, and it cannot be answered except by producing the proof.

**The Same Formula in Sūrat Yūnus**

The parallel verse is worth reading beside this one: *“Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or deny His revelations? Indeed, the wicked will never succeed”* (10:17).

The addition in Sūrat Yūnus supplies what Sūrat al-Kahf leaves implicit — the outcome. Fabrication and denial are named as the two poles of the same wrong, one adding to God and the other subtracting from what He sent, and both are answered with the same verdict: no success. The cave youths reached the same conclusion from inside their own city, before they had left it.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:16

> Since you have distanced yourselves from them and what they worship besides Allah, take refuge in the cave. Your Lord will extend His mercy to you and accommodate you in your ordeal.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**One of Them Takes the Decision**

The speech belongs to a member of the group, not to a prophet and not to a narrator. The exegetical tradition names him Tamlīkhā or Yamlīkhā, and the Christian versions of the same story give a corresponding name, Iamblichus, for the sleeper who was sent into the city.

The Qur'an leaves him anonymous, which is consistent with its treatment of the whole group. What matters is the content of the advice and the fact that it came from inside the group. Young men under threat organised themselves, and one of them articulated the plan.

That detail is easy to miss and worth holding. The narrative does not wait for divine instruction at this point. It shows believers reasoning together about where to go, and the reasoning is sound enough that the verse records it without correction.

There is a further point about leadership. No elder, priest, or prophet speaks. The group's plan comes from one of the youths, and the others follow it. In a story about young people dismissed by their own community, that detail reverses the hierarchy the community assumed.

**Separation Comes First, Refuge Second**

The sentence is built on a condition already fulfilled: since you have distanced yourselves from them and from what they worship besides Allah. The withdrawal is described as accomplished before the cave is mentioned.

The order carries the argument. Refuge makes sense only for people who have already separated in worship; a person still participating in his people's religion has nothing to hide from them. The cave is the consequence of a decision taken earlier, not the cause of it.

The verse therefore distinguishes two separations that are often confused. Withdrawal from shared worship is presented as obligatory. Withdrawal from shared space is presented as tactical, chosen because the first separation had become dangerous to maintain openly.

The wording also fixes responsibility. The distancing is attributed to the youths — *since you have distanced yourselves* — and the refuge follows as a consequence. Nobody is described as having separated them from their people; they did it, and the cave is where that decision leads.

**A Cave Chosen Rather Than Discovered**

The imperative is specific: take refuge in *the* cave. The definite article implies a known place — a shelter the group had identified, presumably in the hills outside the city.

The specificity removes any suggestion that flight was improvised. These young men had considered where they would go if the situation broke, which means they had been living with the possibility for some time.

The choice also reveals what they expected. A cave offers concealment but no defence, no supplies, and no second exit. Choosing it means choosing dependence: whoever controls the mouth of the cave controls what happens inside it. The prayer they had already made is the same statement in different words.

**Two Things Promised in One Sentence**

The assurance has two parts. God will extend mercy to you, and He will make your affair easy — literally, He will provide for you a *mirfaq*, a place of leaning, from a root meaning to rest the arm on a cushion.

The first promise is general and the second is precise. Mercy covers their condition; accommodation covers their circumstances. Together they answer the prayer recorded earlier in the narrative, which asked for mercy from God's presence and right direction in their affair.

The wording is careful about what is not promised. No deliverance from the ordeal is announced, no defeat of the king, no return home. What is promised is mercy inside the ordeal and ease within it — a difference that anyone in a long trial will recognise.

The verb for accommodation deserves a word of its own. A *mirfaq* is what the arm rests on: a cushion, an armrest, something that takes weight off a limb. Applied to an affair, it means the matter will be made bearable rather than removed. The promise is support under a load, not the lifting of the load.

**The Cave the Qur'an Remembers Best**

The most famous cave in Islamic memory belongs to a later event. During the migration to Madinah, the Prophet ﷺ and Abū Bakr sheltered in a cave while the Quraysh searched for them, and the Qur'an records the moment: *“While they both were in the cave, he reassured his companion, ‘Do not worry; Allah is certainly with us.’ So Allah sent down His serenity upon the Prophet”* (9:40).

The commentators connect the two scenes, and the connection is structural rather than sentimental. In both cases believers who have separated from their people's worship enter a cave; in both, pursuit follows; in both, protection comes without any action of their own.

The parallel also clarifies what a cave signifies in this narrative. It is not a hiding place so much as a place where human options have been exhausted and divine action begins.

One difference sharpens the comparison. The Prophet ﷺ and Abū Bakr were in the cave for days and knew it; the youths were in theirs for centuries and did not. Both groups were protected without acting, but only one group was conscious of the protection while it lasted.

**Sanctuary Chosen Over Confrontation**

The verse sanctions withdrawal, and that sanction deserves notice. Nothing here commands the youths to stand their ground, to debate the king publicly, or to accept martyrdom when escape is available.

Islamic tradition does honour martyrdom, and the Qur'an praises those who are killed for their faith. But this verse records believers choosing concealment, and records it approvingly. The condition that makes the choice legitimate is visible in the sentence: the alternative was not a debate but a punishment.

The lesson is one of proportion. Open declaration is required when it can accomplish something; withdrawal is permitted when it can only accomplish the believers' destruction. The youths' judgement on that point is presented without criticism, which makes it a precedent.

The same principle governs the Islamic ruling on migration. A believer who cannot practise his faith openly is expected to leave, and leaving is treated as obedience rather than retreat. The cave is that ruling in narrative form, arrived at by the people it applied to.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:17

> And you would have seen the sun, as it rose, inclining away from their cave to the right, and as it set, declining away from them to the left, while they lay in its open space. That is one of the signs of Allah. Whoever Allah guides is truly guided. But whoever He leaves to stray, you will never find for them a guiding mentor

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Scene Described to a Witness Who Was Not There**

The verse opens in the second person: you would have seen. The addressee is the Prophet ﷺ according to al-Ṭabarī, and an unspecified observer according to others. Neither reading changes the effect, which is to place the reader at the mouth of the cave.

The device is common in the Qur'an's narratives and is not merely decorative. A scene rendered as visible asks the reader to construct it — the angle of the light, the position of the bodies, the space in which they lie. What could have been stated as a fact is instead given as an observation.

There is also an irony in the address. Nobody was there to see this. The protection described happened in an empty landscape, unnoticed by anyone, and the verse supplies the only witness it will ever have.

The effect is to make the unseen visible. Most of what God does for people goes unwitnessed, including by them; this verse narrates one such act as though a spectator stood at the cave's mouth, teaching the reader to look for the same pattern in less visible places.

**The Sun Made to Miss Them**

Two movements are described: at sunrise the light inclines away from the cave to the right, and at sunset it declines away from them to the left. Al-Ṭabarī treats the two phrases as parallel descriptions of one arrangement.

The commentators offer both a physical and a providential reading. Physically, a cave opening toward the north in the northern hemisphere receives no direct sunlight at either solstice extreme; the sun's arc passes to the right in the morning and to the left in the evening. Providentially, the arrangement is attributed to God's action rather than to the youths' choice of shelter.

The two readings do not conflict, and the verse does not choose between them. What matters is the result: bodies preserved for centuries were not baked by the sun, and the narrative treats that as something arranged rather than lucky.

**An Open Space With Air Moving Through It**

The phrase translated as its open space describes a broad area within the cave — a *fahwā*, an open expanse. The commentators read it as indicating a cave with a wide mouth, allowing air and cool breezes to reach the sleepers.

The detail answers an objection a hearer would raise immediately. Bodies sealed in a closed space for centuries would decay from damp and stagnant air. A spacious cave with through-draft addresses the problem, and the verse mentions it in the same breath as the sun.

The Qur'an treats shade as a blessing in its own right, listed among the gifts of the created order: *“And Allah has provided you shade out of what He created, and has given you shelter in the mountains”* (16:81). The youths receive both gifts at once — shade and a shelter in the mountains — without asking for either.

The verse is also a quiet rebuke to the idea that God's care is reserved for dramatic rescue. Nothing here intervenes in history or overturns a kingdom. The sun simply misses them, and the air moves. Care of that kind is easy to overlook precisely because it looks like weather.

**The Narrative Stops to Label Its Own Sign**

Halfway through the scene the verse interrupts itself: that is one of the signs of Allah. The label is unusual. Most narratives in the Qur'an leave the reader to draw the conclusion; here the conclusion is stated inside the description.

The interruption has a purpose. The scene could easily be received as a curious detail — a lucky orientation, a well-ventilated cave. Naming it a sign fixes its status as evidence and prevents the reading that reduces it to geography.

The phrasing is also measured. *One of* the signs: this protection is not the greatest wonder in the story, still less in the Book. It is one item in a catalogue, which is exactly how the sūrah's opening question framed the whole narrative.

The placement is significant too. The label sits between the physical description and the statement about guidance, linking them. What the reader has just been shown is not a curiosity but evidence, and the evidence is meant to support the conclusion about guidance that follows.

**Guidance and Straying Stated as the Conclusion**

The last two sentences step outside the scene entirely. Whoever Allah guides is truly guided, and whoever He leaves to stray will find no guiding mentor.

The connection to the cave is not immediately obvious, and the commentators explain it in two ways. One reads the sentences as applying to the youths: they were guided, and their guidance produced the protection just described. The other reads them as applying to their persecutors, who were left to stray and found no one to redirect them.

Both readings are supported by the Qur'an's use of the same formula elsewhere: *“Whoever Allah guides is truly guided. And whoever He leaves to stray, they are the ˹true˺ losers”* (7:178), and *“Whoever Allah guides is truly guided. And whoever He leaves to stray, you will find no guardians for them besides Him”* (17:97). In a story about two groups — protected sleepers and a pursuing king — the formula applies to both, and that is precisely why it closes the scene.

**Shade as a Standing Blessing**

The physical detail of this verse belongs to a wider Qur'anic theme. Shade is described as extended by God and guided by the sun: *“Have you not seen how your Lord extends the shade—He could have simply made it ˹remain˺ still if He so willed—then We make the sun its guide”* (25:45), *“causing the shade to retreat gradually?”* (25:46).

In the Garden, shade becomes permanent: *“and We will place them under a vast shade”* (4:57). The cave is the intermediate case — shade arranged for a specific purpose, for a specific group, for a specific number of years — and the verse treats it as an instance of the same generosity.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:18

> And you would have thought they were awake, though they were asleep. We turned them over, to the right and left, while their dog stretched his forelegs at the entrance. Had you looked at them, you would have certainly fled away from them, filled with horror

**Expanded Commentary**

**Asleep, and Looking Awake**

The verse states a contradiction and leaves it standing. An observer would have taken them for waking men; they were asleep. The commentators offer two explanations — that their eyes remained open, and that the periodic turning gave them the appearance of movement — and some suggest the turning occurred once or twice a year.

The paradox is not a puzzle to be solved but a description of an intermediate condition. Ordinary sleep is recognisable; these bodies were not. Whatever the mechanism, the narrative insists that appearance and reality diverged completely, and that the divergence was part of the arrangement rather than an accident of it.

There is a further implication. A group of men appearing awake in a cave is a group nobody approaches. The deception described in the first sentence does protective work that the rest of the verse makes explicit.

The wording also refuses to explain itself. The Qur'an does not say whether their eyes were open, whether they breathed visibly, or whether they shifted position. It states the effect on an observer and leaves the mechanism alone, which is consistent with its treatment of every other detail in this account.

**Turned Over So the Earth Would Not Consume Them**

The turning has a stated agent and an intelligible purpose. God turned them to the right and to the left, and al-Ṭabarī explains the reason: to prevent their bodies from being consumed by contact with the ground.

The detail is medically plausible and theologically pointed. A body left in one position develops pressure damage; the Qur'an attributes the prevention of that damage to a deliberate act. Nothing in the description suggests the youths turned themselves, which would have been impossible in the depth of sleep described earlier.

The verse therefore answers the last remaining objection to a centuries-long sleep. Food and water were removed by the sleep itself; heat was removed by the sun's deflection; damp and decay are answered here. The narrative is systematically closing every gap a hearer might find.

**The Dog at the Entrance**

An animal is included in the scene, and its inclusion has generated more discussion than any other detail in the story. The reports vary: some describe a hunting dog the youths brought with them, some a sheepdog belonging to a shepherd who followed them, and some a dog that spoke to them on the road, saying that it loved the lovers of God and would keep watch while they slept.

One strand of commentary removes the animal altogether. Al-Ṭabarī records the view that *kalb* here was the name of a man — their cook, according to some — and al-Zamakhsharī records the reading *kālib*, a dog-keeper, in which case the limbs stretched at the entrance would be arms.

The literal reading is the stronger, and the reason is grammatical: the word for forelegs used here belongs to animals. What the detail contributes is worth stating plainly. A creature outside the covenant of prophecy is included in the protection, stationed at the threshold, described in the same breath as the believers.

**Terror Placed Over Them as a Guard**

The final sentence addresses the reader directly: had you come upon them, you would have turned and fled, filled with horror. The commentators explain that God clothed them in a fearsome appearance so that no one would touch or disturb them.

The protection is now complete and layered. The cave concealed them, the sun avoided them, the turning preserved them, and their appearance repelled anyone who found them. Not one of these measures required anything from the sleepers.

The verse also concedes something a pious reader might find uncomfortable: these were not beautiful to look at. Sanctity in the narrative is not aesthetic. What made them holy made them frightening, and the narrator says so without softening it.

The horror is functional rather than punitive. Nothing suggests the youths were being punished or marked. The fear they inspire is described as a feature of their protection, added to the others, and directed at anyone who might disturb a scene that had been arranged without them.

**Sleep as the Image of the Interval**

The state described in this verse is neither life as it is normally lived nor death, and the Qur'an has a way of speaking about exactly that region. Sleep is called a taking of the soul: *“He is the One Who calls back your souls by night and knows what you do by day, then revives you daily to complete your appointed term”* (6:60).

The same idea appears in Sūrat al-Zumar, where death and sleep are handled as one process with two outcomes (39:42). Sleep is therefore a minor death, and waking a minor resurrection — which is why the cave narrative works as an argument about the greater one.

Islamic theology names the interval *barzakh*, the barrier or isthmus between two things. The sleepers occupy it literally: alive but not living, dead to the world but not dead. Their centuries are the Qur'an's picture of what every person enters at death and leaves at resurrection.

The picture has a second use in the sūrah. A reader who has accepted that people can be held in an interval for three hundred years has already conceded the difficulty of denying a general resurrection. The narrative argument is conducted by example rather than by syllogism.

**A Saying About Sleep and Death**

A line often quoted in this connection runs: people are asleep, and when they die they wake. It is commonly circulated as a prophetic ḥadīth, and that attribution does not hold. The ḥadīth scholars, including al-Sakhāwī in *al-Maqāṣid al-Ḥasanah* and al-Qārī in *al-Asrār al-Marfūʿah*, record it as a saying of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib rather than of the Prophet ﷺ.

The correction matters here for a reason of method as much as accuracy. The verse itself supplies what the saying expresses: the sleepers were not dead, and their waking was not a resurrection. Keeping the two sources distinct — a verse that states a fact, and a companion's saying that draws an analogy — keeps the argument honest.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:19

> And so We awakened them so that they might question one another. One of them exclaimed, “How long have you remained ˹asleep˺?” Some replied, “Perhaps a day, or part of a day.” They said ˹to one another˺, “Your Lord knows best how long you have remained. So send one of you with these silver coins of yours to the city, and let him find which food is the purest, and then bring you provisions from it. Let him be ˹exceptionally˺ cautious, and do not let him give you away

**Expanded Commentary**

**Awakened in Order to Ask a Question**

The purpose of the awakening is stated before the awakening is described: so that they might question one another. The narrative does not say they were woken to be fed, to be delivered, or to be shown to their people. It says they were woken to talk.

The conversation is the test. The verse that raised them announced that God would show which of the two groups estimated their stay better, and this verse administers the examination — not by an external inspector but by a question one of them asks the others.

The arrangement deserves a moment's attention. The youths are not told they are being tested. They wake, they wonder, they ask, and their answers are the result. The examination is conducted entirely inside their own ordinary conversation, which is how the Qur'an generally describes divine assessment.

**A Day, or Part of a Day**

The answer given is cautious and reasonable: we have remained a day, or part of a day. Nobody claims certainty, and the hedge between the two estimates is preserved in the wording rather than resolved.

Their reasoning would have been bodily. They had lain down, apparently, in the evening or at some point in the day, and they woke without the stiffness, hunger, or disorientation of a longer interval. Nothing in their experience pointed beyond a night.

The hedge in their answer is itself informative. They offer two estimates rather than one and decline to choose between them, which is what people do when the evidence supports a range but not a point.

The estimate is therefore not stupidity but evidence-based judgement, and the narrative does not mock it. A reader should notice how rarely any text allows a group of people to be wrong in a way that is entirely defensible. The Qur'an does, and it records their words without correction.

**The Referral That Ends the Discussion**

The reply that follows does not offer a better number. It offers a better kind of answer: your Lord knows best how long you have remained. The discussion closes there, and no further estimate is attempted.

This is the estimate the earlier verse was measuring, and it is better than a guess for a specific reason. Faced with a question that lies wholly outside anyone's experience, the group refuses to manufacture a figure. They state what they know — a day, or part of a day — and then hand the matter to the one who has the information.

The verse is often read as a lesson in humility, which it is. Its sharper edge is epistemic. It distinguishes between admitting ignorance and filling a gap with confidence, and it presents the first as the mature response rather than the weaker one.

The same verse then moves immediately to practical matters, and that move matters. Referring the unknown to God does not suspend action. The group settles the question it cannot answer, and proceeds to arrange food.

**Silver Coins and the Purest Food**

Two practical instructions follow, and both are specific. The first concerns money: send one of you with these silver coins of yours. The word *wariq* denotes coined silver — currency already minted, not bullion — and the demonstrative *these* implies money the youths actually carried when they fled.

The second concerns what to buy: let him see which of them has the purest food. The word *azkā* carries both senses of purity — ritually lawful and physically wholesome — and the commentators read it as covering both. Even in flight, the group's standard for food is not convenience.

The pairing is quietly instructive. A group that has just woken from a centuries-long sleep and does not know where it is begins by arranging lawful provisions. Priorities survive disorientation, and the verse shows them surviving.

There is also a detail about community in the instruction. The food is to be brought back for all of them — *bring you provisions from it* — and one man is sent for the whole group. Nobody disperses into the city separately, which is consistent with the caution that follows.

**Caution Issued as a Command**

The final instruction is emphatic: let him be *extremely* cautious — the verb *yatalaṭṭaf*, from a root meaning subtlety and gentleness, in an intensified form — and let him not make anyone aware of you.

The command is about manner as much as about outcome. The young man is not simply told to be careful; he is told to be delicate, to move in a way that draws no attention. The same root describes the gentle handling of a difficult matter.

The verse treats concealment as a legitimate and even obligatory strategy. No criticism attaches to it, and no hint suggests that openness would have been more virtuous. The group's danger is real, and the instruction is proportioned to it.

**Coins From Another Century**

The Christian versions of the story preserve a detail that illuminates this verse. When the sleeper enters the city to buy food, he pays with coinage from the reign of Decius, and the shopkeeper, assuming he has found a buried hoard, reports him to the authorities. Gregory of Tours records the scene, and the Syriac versions preserve it in the same shape.

The detail explains why the Qur'an mentions the coins at all. Old currency in a changed city is a witness: it dates the bearer without his saying a word. The verse's reference to *these silver coins of yours* is not domestic realism but the mechanism by which the story becomes known.

What the Qur'an does not do is narrate the consequence. The coins are mentioned, the caution is commanded, and the outcome is left to the verses that follow.

The restraint is characteristic. The sūrah consistently declines to supply details that do not bear on its argument, and here it withholds the most dramatic moment in the Christian versions — the arrest, the crowd, the discovery — in favour of the instruction that preceded it.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:20

> For, indeed, if they find out about you, they will stone you ˹to death˺, or force you back into their faith, and then you will never succeed.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Outcomes, and the Worse Is Not Death**

The youths name two things their people will do if they are discovered, and the order of the two is the verse's argument. First, stoning. Second, being returned to the community's religion. Death is listed first and forced apostasy second, yet the concluding verdict — you will never succeed — attaches to the sequence as a whole and falls hardest on the second outcome.

Anyone who has read martyrdom accounts will expect the reverse ordering. A group of believers facing execution would normally treat death as the worst possibility. These young men treat compulsory return to idolatry as the danger that ends them.

The reason is visible in what they say elsewhere in the narrative. Their declaration had been that calling on another god would be an outrageous lie. Being forced to utter that lie is, in their own assessment, a fate with consequences beyond the body.

The order also reveals what they understood their situation to be. Death would end the test; compelled worship would decide it. The distinction only makes sense to people who believe that something is being measured, and the youths plainly believe that.

**Stoning Named Specifically**

The punishment is *rajm*, stoning — a public, communal execution in which the crowd is the instrument. The choice of word is not incidental. Stoning is what a society does to someone it regards as a polluter rather than a criminal; it requires no court and no single executioner.

In the context the narrative supplies, that is precisely what the youths would have been. A group of young men from respectable families who had abandoned the city's gods were not offenders against a law but contaminators of a cult, and the punishment for that category was communal.

The Christian versions describe the same hostility in a different form: the emperor, finding the youths hidden, orders the cave sealed so that they die inside. Both accounts agree on the essential fact — that the community's response was to eliminate them rather than to argue with them.

**Never Succeeding as the Final Verdict**

The sentence closes with *lan tufliḥū*, you will never succeed. The negative particle *lan* denies the possibility permanently rather than temporarily, and *falāḥ* is a comprehensive word: success, flourishing, deliverance, the attainment of what one was aiming at.

The Qur'an uses the phrase as a standing verdict on categories of conduct, and it never applies it lightly. Its force here is that the loss described is total. Stoning takes a life; forced return to a false religion takes the thing the life was for.

The youths are reasoning, not preaching. Their calculation is that the second outcome is worse, and the calculation shapes the caution commanded in the previous verse. Prudence in the narrative is not timidity but arithmetic done correctly.

The finality of *lan* is worth marking. The Qur'an elsewhere uses this particle for permanent negation, and its use by these young men shows that they understood the stakes in those terms — not a setback to be recovered from, but a state with no exit.

**What Coercion Excuses and What It Does Not**

The Qur'an addresses the situation of compelled belief elsewhere, and the ruling is nuanced. *“Whoever disbelieves in Allah after their belief—not those who are forced while their hearts are firm in faith, but those who embrace disbelief wholeheartedly—they will be condemned by Allah and suffer a tremendous punishment”* (16:106).

The verse distinguishes coerced utterance from embraced disbelief and excuses the first. A person forced to speak words of unbelief while his heart holds firm is not condemned for the words.

That ruling raises a question about the cave youths. If coercion is excused, why do they treat forced return as ruin? The answer lies in the wording of 16:106 itself, which excuses the coerced but condemns those who open their hearts to disbelief. Compulsion is a real danger precisely because it does not always stop at the tongue. The youths' fear is not of a legal verdict but of themselves.

**Prudence as a Religious Obligation**

Read together with the verse before it, this passage establishes concealment as a duty in defined conditions. The instruction to be extremely cautious is not advice to the faint-hearted; it is a command issued by believers to a believer, grounded in a clear assessment of consequences.

The condition that legitimises it is stated in the verse. The alternative to concealment is not debate but destruction, and the destruction includes a religious component. Where the choice is between a hidden faith and a compelled public apostasy, the Qur'an's narrative endorses the hidden faith.

The same logic governs the wider Islamic rules on migration and concealment. A believer who cannot practise openly is instructed to move; if he cannot move, he is not required to be discovered.

The verse also gives the reason rather than only the rule. The youths state what will happen if they are found, and the instruction to be cautious follows from that statement. Religious obligation in the narrative is presented with its grounds attached, which is how the Qur'an generally handles commands of this kind.

**The Same Distinction in Sūrat al-Naḥl**

The parallel is exact and worth reading beside this verse: *“not those who are forced while their hearts are firm in faith”* (16:106). Both passages separate the state of the heart from the words extracted from it, and both refuse to treat the two as identical.

The difference between them is instructive. Sūrat al-Naḥl states a ruling for someone already under compulsion; Sūrat al-Kahf records believers arranging not to reach that point. The ruling and the precaution belong together, and the cave narrative supplies the second half.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:21

> That is how We caused them to be discovered so that their people might know that Allah’s promise ˹of resurrection˺ is true and that there is no doubt about the Hour. When the people disputed with each other about the case of the youth ˹after their death˺, some proposed, “Build a structure around them. Their Lord knows best about them.” Those who prevailed in the matter said, “We will surely build a place of worship over them.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Discovered So That Something Would Be Known**

The discovery is not an accident inside the story; it is its stated purpose. God caused them to be found *so that their people might know* two things: that God's promise of resurrection is true, and that there is no doubt about the Hour.

Both objects of knowledge concern the future rather than the past. The event is meant to change what a community believes about what is coming, not merely to add a remarkable episode to its history. A miracle that left belief untouched would have failed by the verse's own criterion.

The wording also fixes the audience. Their people — the city, not the world — are the intended witnesses. The sign was arranged for a specific group of people at a specific moment, which is how the Qur'an generally describes divine evidence: addressed, situated, and meant to be checked by those who receive it.

**What the Reports Say Happened Next**

The exegetical tradition fills in the sequence. A shepherd looking for shelter cleared the cave's entrance and the youths woke. The one sent into the city gave himself away with an old silver coin; the townspeople learned from him what had happened and were led to the cave by a king of their time, described in the reports as righteous.

The context the reports supply is a dispute about resurrection — specifically, whether the body rises with the soul or the soul alone. The Syriac versions of the Christian legend preserve the same setting, naming a bishop who denied the resurrection of the body and treating the sleepers' awakening as the answer to him.

The Qur'an itself states only the outcome: their people came to know. It records neither the king's name nor the details of the argument, which is consistent with its handling of every other detail in this account.

**What Was Dug Up at Ephesus**

The site traditionally identified with the story has been excavated, and the findings are worth recording. On the slope of Mount Pion — Panayır Dağı, above the ruins of Ephesus near modern Selçuk — Austrian excavations in 1926 to 1928 uncovered a church built over a grotto, surrounded by several hundred graves dating to the fifth and sixth centuries.

The graves are the significant find. People wanted to be buried beside the sleepers, and the inscriptions found on the walls and in the graves invoke them by name. Within a few generations of the story's earliest written versions, this hillside had already become the address of the event.

None of this establishes that the youths existed. It establishes that the belief was old, local, and strong enough to shape where a community chose to be buried — which is what the Qur'an describes when it reports the dispute over what to build at the site.

**A Structure, or a Place of Worship**

The dispute recorded in the verse is about commemoration. One party proposes a structure built around them; the party that prevails proposes a place of worship. Al-Ṭabarī reports that a building was erected which later became a place of prayer.

The two proposals are not equally weighted in the verse's grammar. The first is reported as a suggestion — *some proposed* — with the concession *their Lord knows best about them* attached to it. The second is reported as a decision by those who prevailed.

That asymmetry has been read in different ways. Some commentators take the first group to be the believers, cautious about what they build, and the second to be those with power. Others read both as believers disagreeing about form. The verse does not resolve it, and the unresolved form is itself informative: the Qur'an records the disagreement without endorsing either side.

**A Reported Speech, Not a Permission**

The words *we will surely build a place of worship over them* are the speech of a group in a story, not an instruction from God. The distinction is essential, because the verse has sometimes been cited as if it authorised building over graves.

Later Islamic practice reads it the other way. ʿĀʾishah reported that during his final illness the Prophet ﷺ said: *“Allah has cursed the Jews and Christians, who have taken the graves of their prophets as places of worship”* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1390; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 529), and she added that his own grave was kept from a prominent place for exactly that reason.

The two texts do not conflict once the category of each is fixed. One narrates what people said; the other rules on what believers may do. Reading the first as a ruling requires ignoring that it is quoted speech inside a narrative.

**A Promise Confirmed by a Body**

The verse's stated purpose explains the narrative's preoccupation with bodies. The sun was deflected so they would not decay. They were turned so the earth would not consume them. Their appearance was made fearsome so no one would disturb them. Every measure concerns the physical preservation of the group.

That emphasis is only necessary if the body matters to the point being made. A story proving the survival of souls would not need to bother with shade. The townspeople's dispute over resurrection, reported in the same verse, confirms that this was the live question.

The argument is therefore concrete: what can hold bodies unchanged for three centuries can reassemble them at the end.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:22

> Some will say, “They were three, their dog was the fourth,” while others will say, “They were five, their dog was the sixth,” ˹only˺ guessing blindly. And others will say, “They were seven and their dog was the eighth.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “My Lord knows best their ˹exact˺ number. Only a few people know as well.” So do not argue about them except with sure knowledge, nor consult any of those ˹who debate˺ about them

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Numbers, and the Qur'an Refuses to Choose**

The verse lists three estimates and gives none of them its own verdict. Three, with the dog as the fourth. Five, with the dog as the sixth. Seven, with the dog as the eighth. Each is reported in the speech of others — *some will say*, *others will say* — and the Qur'an stands outside all three.

The reporting is exact rather than casual. Every figure is given with its dog, which is how the estimates were actually circulated, and the pattern of odd numbers followed by the animal is preserved in all three. Whoever compiled these claims kept their form.

The refusal to settle the matter is the verse's real content. A text concerned only with establishing a fact would name the number. This one records the dispute, labels the method that produced it, and then closes the question.

The listing has a second effect. By setting the three estimates side by side, the verse lets the reader see that they were generated by the same procedure: someone counted a group he had never seen. The disagreement between them is not evidence that one is right, but evidence that none of them rests on observation.

**Only the Middle Estimate Is Called a Guess**

The disparaging phrase — guessing blindly, *rajman bi-l-ghayb*, literally throwing at the unseen — is attached to the second estimate and to no other. The commentators noticed the asymmetry and offered two explanations.

One reads it as a signal: the absence of the label from the third figure indicates that seven is the correct number, since the Qur'an would not leave an error unmarked. The other reads the placement as rhetorical rather than informative, the label applying to all three but inserted where the list needs a comment.

The second reading is stronger on the text's own terms. The verse immediately denies that anyone knows the number except a few, which would be a strange thing to say if the correct figure had just been identified. And the phrase *only a few people know as well* functions to close speculation rather than to endorse one of the three options listed before it.

**An Argument Forbidden Except on Grounds**

The instruction that follows is narrower than a blanket prohibition. Do not argue about them *except with sure knowledge*. Debate is not banned; debate without evidence is.

The distinction matters for how the verse has been used. It is often read as forbidding discussion of the story altogether, which is not what it says. What it forbids is continuing a dispute in which no party possesses grounds — precisely the condition described in the first half of the verse.

The rule generalises. Where a matter lies outside anyone's access, the honest position is to say so and stop. The Qur'an applies that rule to itself here, declining to supply a detail it has not been given.

**A Referral That Is Not an Answer**

Do not consult any of them about them. The pronouns are deliberately dense: do not ask the people who debate this matter *about* this matter. Al-Ṭabarī reads the referent as the People of the Book, the source to whom the Quraysh had directed their question in the first place.

The instruction completes an arc. The challenge had been to test the Prophet ﷺ against the knowledge of the Jewish scholars of Madinah. The answer arrives, and with it a ruling that those scholars are no longer the reference point. The revealed account has replaced the consulted authority.

That is a stronger claim than it looks. It says that on this subject the questioners have been asking the wrong people, and that the correct answer has now been given in a form that requires no further enquiry.

The instruction also protects the Prophet ﷺ from a trap. Every additional question referred outward keeps the authority outside the revelation, and each answer becomes one more statement to be tested against another tradition. Closing the referral ends that sequence.

**The Unseen Has a Single Owner**

Underneath the whole verse sits a principle stated elsewhere without qualification: *“With Him are the keys of the unseen—no one knows them except Him. And He knows what is in the land and sea. Not even a leaf falls without His knowledge”* (6:59).

The principle explains why the number is withheld. If knowledge of the unseen belongs to God alone, then a human estimate of it — three, five, seven — has no standing however confidently it is stated. The verse is not being coy about a fact; it is enforcing a boundary.

The boundary also protects the story's purpose. The narrative exists to demonstrate resurrection, and its argument does not depend on a headcount. Every detail the sūrah supplies is one the argument needs; this one is not.

The verse thus performs a kind of triage on its own material. What serves the purpose is given at length — the flight, the prayer, the sleep, the sun, the discovery. What serves curiosity is withheld, and the withholding is stated rather than left as a silence.

**"Allah Willing" Attached to the End**

The verse closes with an instruction to say *Allah Willing*, and the phrase is left hanging without a stated object. What follows picks it up immediately, and the sequence is deliberate: the reader is told to attach God's will to his statements before being shown how.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:23

> And never say of anything, “I will definitely do this tomorrow,”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Prohibition Aimed at Certainty, Not at Planning**

The verse forbids a specific sentence rather than a specific action. What is prohibited is saying *I will definitely do this tomorrow* — a statement about one's own future delivered in the indicative, with the certainty built into the verb.

Planning is not the target. The Qur'an elsewhere describes people arranging journeys, harvests, and battles without criticism. What is criticised here is the grammatical form of self-sufficiency: a future asserted as though the speaker controlled it.

The word *definitely* in the translation carries the weight. Arabic marks the certainty in the verb form itself, and the prohibition attaches to that marker. Remove the certainty and the statement becomes a statement of intention, which the verse does not touch.

The object of the prohibition is equally broad: *of anything*. No category of future act is exempted — not a journey, not a business deal, not a religious obligation. The rule concerns the form of the claim rather than the content of the plan.

**Fifteen Days of Silence**

The occasion is preserved in the reports about this sūrah's revelation. The Quraysh had put three questions to the Prophet ﷺ — the youths of the cave, the traveller who reached the east and west, and the spirit — and he answered that he would respond the following day. Revelation did not come when he expected.

The delay stretched, in the most commonly transmitted account, to fifteen days. During that period the Quraysh treated his silence as disproof, and the distress is visible in the sūrah's earlier verse about grieving himself to death over their denial.

Read in that context, the instruction is not a general etiquette rule dropped into the narrative. It addresses the exact sentence the Prophet ﷺ had spoken in public, at the moment when its failure was being used against him.

The timing sharpens the point further. The correction arrives with the answer, not before it. The Prophet ﷺ is given what he promised and told, in the same revelation, what he should have said when he promised it.

**What the Exception Actually Asserts**

The formula the verse requires is short, and its meaning is precise: *if Allah so wills*. It does not express doubt about one's intention. It states a fact about causation — that between an intention and its execution stands a will that is not the speaker's.

The claim is metaphysical rather than pious. A person who says he will do something tomorrow is asserting a chain of events: that he will be alive, that his circumstances will permit it, that nothing will intervene. Every link in that chain lies outside his control, and the formula acknowledges this in four words.

The Qur'an states the same principle as a plain fact elsewhere: *“No soul knows what it will earn for tomorrow, and no soul knows in what land it will die”* (31:34). The exception clause is that verse compressed into a phrase.

Notice what the formula does not do. It does not weaken the intention or license inaction. A person who says he will do something tomorrow, God willing, has still committed to doing it; the qualification concerns who makes the commitment effective, not whether it is sincere.

**A Correction Addressed to the Messenger Himself**

The instruction is given to the Prophet ﷺ directly, in a sūrah that is answering a challenge to his prophethood. He is corrected in the same text that vindicates him.

That combination is unusual and has been used as an argument about the text's origin. A composition produced to establish a man's authority would be unlikely to record his error at the precise moment his authority was under public examination. The correction appears where it is least convenient for its subject.

The Qur'an handles its messenger this way repeatedly — correcting him on the prisoner of Badr, on the blind man who interrupted him, on the matter of Zayd's wife. The pattern is consistent enough that this verse reads as part of a rule rather than an exception to one.

**The Practice the Verse Founded**

The formula entered Muslim speech as a matter of course, and its use is not confined to formal oaths. Any statement about the future carries it, in conversation, in writing, in the announcement of a meeting.

Jurists discussed the boundaries. Where the statement is an oath, the exception must generally be attached at once to take legal effect, and a broken oath carries its own expiation. Where the statement is ordinary speech, the formula is a recognition rather than a condition.

The verse itself is concerned with the second case. It is training a habit of mind, not drafting a contract, and its object is that the speaker never loses awareness of who holds the future.

**Solomon's Oath in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī**

The clearest illustration of the principle comes from the Prophet ﷺ himself, who told a story about a prophet who forgot the formula. Abū Hurayrah reported that Solomon said he would go around to his wives that night, each of whom would bear a child who would fight in God's cause; he was told to say *if Allah wills*, and he did not (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6720).

The outcome is given in the report: none conceived except one, who bore a half-formed child, and the Prophet ﷺ added that had Solomon said the formula, his purpose would have been achieved.

The story supplies what the verse leaves implicit. The omission is not a linguistic slip but a claim about control, and the report shows what follows when the claim is wrong.

The verse is therefore not a rule about grammar. It is a rule about how a person holds the future, and the forgetting it names is the ordinary kind.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:24

> without adding, “if Allah so wills!” But if you forget, then remember your Lord, and say, “I trust my Lord will guide me to what is more right than this.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Completes the Sentence**

The verse supplies the phrase the previous one required, and its position is grammatical rather than incidental. The prohibition was on saying *I will definitely do this tomorrow*; the exception attaches to the sentence itself, converting an assertion into a conditional.

The effect is a change of speaker. A statement qualified by *if Allah so wills* no longer claims that the speaker will bring the event about; it states an intention and places its fulfilment elsewhere. The same words, with four added, mean something different.

The formula is also portable. It attaches to any statement about the future without altering the statement's content, which is why it survives in ordinary speech rather than remaining a liturgical formula. A merchant, a traveller, and a man promising to visit a neighbour all use the same four words.

**Forgetting Is Assumed, Not Condemned**

The second clause anticipates failure: *but if you forget, then remember your Lord*. The verse does not treat the omission as a sin requiring punishment or as evidence of a weak faith. It treats it as something that happens, and provides a remedy.

The remedy is stated in the simplest possible terms. Remember. No expiation is demanded at this point, no reproach is attached, and no distinction is drawn between the careful and the careless.

The assumption matters. A moral system that treated every lapse as fatal would produce anxiety rather than correction. This verse assumes lapses and directs the lapse toward its opposite — renewed remembrance — without dwelling on the failure itself.

The wording is also personal. The instruction is to remember *your Lord*, not to repeat a formula correctly. What is being restored is a relationship that was momentarily unattended, and the formula is the means of restoring it rather than the substance of the act.

**When the Reminder May Be Made**

The commentators differ on the timing, and the disagreement has practical consequences. One position holds that the formula should be uttered as soon as the omission is noticed, ideally while the speaker is still in the same place and situation in which he made the original statement. The other permits it considerably later.

The grounds differ in kind. The first treats the exception as attached to the original sentence, so that delay weakens the connection between the two. The second treats the remembrance as an act of the heart that loses nothing by delay, since its object is God rather than the sentence.

Both positions agree on one point that the verse makes explicit: the person who realises his omission must not let it pass. Al-Ṭabarī adds that where the statement was an oath, expiation for breaking it may still be owed regardless of when the formula is spoken.

The disagreement is therefore about form rather than about duty. Both sides require the remembrance; they differ on how tightly it must be bound to the original words. The verse itself fixes only the obligation and leaves the timing to judgement.

**"More Right Than This"**

The phrase the speaker is told to add has been read in two directions.

The first takes it as the content of the remembrance itself: on remembering, one says that God may guide him to what is more correct than the statement he has just made. The second reads it as a prayer of the Prophet ﷺ for a greater sign than the account of the cave youths — a request that the evidence of his prophethood be increased beyond what he had just been given.

The first reading fits the immediate instruction better, since the verse is telling the speaker what to say. The second has the advantage of connecting the verse to its context in a sūrah that has just answered a public challenge, and al-Bayḍāwī and others preferred it.

The two are not strictly incompatible. A man who has just been shown that his certainty was misplaced can reasonably ask for guidance toward something better than the confidence he had displayed.

**The Same Remedy in Sūrat al-Anʿām**

The parallel is close enough to be instructive: *“Should Satan make you forget, then once you remember, do not ˹continue to˺ sit with the wrongdoing people”* (6:68). A different subject, the same structure — forgetfulness anticipated, remembrance prescribed, no reproach attached.

The pairing establishes a rule in the Qur'an's treatment of lapses. Forgetting is not treated as a state one remains in; it is treated as an interruption that ends at the moment of remembering, and the instruction is always about what to do next.

The consistency across two Makkan sūrahs suggests a settled approach rather than a local accommodation. The believer is expected to fail at attention and to recover from it, repeatedly, and the text is written for someone who will.

**A Sentence That Ends on Trust**

The verse closes with a statement of trust rather than a rule: *I trust my Lord will guide me*. The Arabic particle carries expectation rather than certainty, which keeps the sentence inside the boundary the verse has just drawn.

Even the remedy is phrased conditionally. A man instructed to qualify his statements about tomorrow does not finish by making an unqualified claim about God's action; he says he trusts, and leaves the outcome where the verse has placed it all along.

The shape of the sentence is the lesson. Nothing in it asserts that the speaker controls what comes next, and the trust it expresses is placed in guidance rather than in outcome.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:25

> They had remained in their cave for three hundred years, adding nine

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Number Arrives After the Refusal**

The verse before this one forbade argument about the youths and declared that only God knows their number. This one supplies a figure. The juxtaposition is deliberate and does the work of an argument.

What is given is a duration, not a headcount. The question the Qur'an refused to answer was how many they were; the question it answers is how long they stayed. The distinction is not arbitrary. The number has no bearing on the story's purpose, while the length is the whole of its evidential force.

The placement also ends a discussion. After three estimates and a refusal to adjudicate, a single figure is stated flatly, in the indicative, with no attribution to anyone's opinion and no hedge attached.

The shift in register is worth noticing. The surrounding verses are full of reported speech — some say, others say — and this one contains none. Whatever the figure is, it is not being relayed; it is being asserted.

**Why the Number Is Stated So Oddly**

The phrasing is *three hundred years, adding nine*, not three hundred and nine. The commentators have discussed the form, and the most common explanation is that the addition is what makes the count precise: a round figure followed by its correction.

There is a second effect. The construction forces the reader to perform the addition rather than receive a total. A number that must be computed is harder to dismiss as a careless tradition, and the phrasing invites the very calculation that explains it.

Some early readings treated the figure as a report of what the People of the Book said rather than as God's own statement, on the grounds that the following verse says God knows best how long they stayed. That reading is coherent but not necessary, since the two statements can stand together without contradiction.

**Three Hundred Solar Years Are Three Hundred and Nine Lunar Years**

The classical explanation of the nine is calendrical, and the arithmetic holds. Three hundred solar years of 365.2422 days total 109,572.7 days; divided by the lunar year of 354.367 days, that is 309.2 lunar years. The nine-year difference is not an addition to the period but the same period counted in a different system.

The explanation fits the audience. The Arabs kept a lunar calendar, and a period remembered in solar terms by another community would arrive in Arabic speech with the difference spelled out. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qushayrī both record this reading.

It also explains why the figure is expressed as a sum. A scribe copying "three hundred and nine" would have no reason to split it; a translator reconciling two calendars would.

The explanation has an additional advantage. It accounts for why both numbers appear in traditions about the story — a period remembered as three hundred by one community and three hundred and nine by another is one period, not two competing claims.

**A Figure That Does Not Close the Matter**

The next verse instructs the Prophet ﷺ to say that God knows best how long they stayed, and commentators have handled the apparent tension in three ways.

The first treats the figure as the number the People of the Book gave, with God's knowledge left superior to it. The second takes the 309 years as the actual duration of the sleep and reads God's greater knowledge as referring to the time the youths spent in the cave after their death. The third sees the figure as settling an existing dispute, with the following statement confirming the settlement.

The second reading has the advantage of taking both statements at face value. The first requires the Qur'an to report a number it does not endorse, which is unusual for its handling of facts. The third is the simplest but explains least.

What all three readings share is the priority of divine knowledge over human calculation, which is the point the two verses make together.

**Why the Length Is the Whole Argument**

The story's evidential purpose depends on duration, and the verse supplies it. A group of young men preserved unharmed for a night proves nothing about resurrection. The same group preserved for three centuries proves that the one who held them there can hold the dead and return them.

The narrative had already built toward this. The sun's deflection, the turning of their bodies, the dog at the entrance — each detail removed an objection to a long sleep. The figure now states how long the objections had to be answered for.

The verse is short because it needs to be. Everything the account required was established earlier; this line supplies the magnitude, and the argument that follows in the next verse rests on nothing else.

**The Same Estimate After a Hundred Years**

The Qur'an offers a precise parallel. A man is caused to die for a century and is then raised: *“Allah asked, ‘How long have you remained ˹in this state˺?’ He replied, ‘Perhaps a day or part of a day.’ Allah said, ‘No! You have remained here for a hundred years!’”* (2:259).

The parallel runs on three levels. The sleeper's estimate is a day or part of a day in both accounts. The correction comes from outside his own experience in both. And in both, the purpose is stated: the man is made a sign for humanity, as the youths were made known so that their people would know God's promise is true.

Set beside that verse, the figure here is not an isolated curiosity. It is the second instance of a single argument the Qur'an makes about time, sleep, and the return of the dead.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:26

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Allah knows best how long they stayed. With Him ˹alone˺ is ˹the knowledge of˺ the unseen of the heavens and the earth. How perfectly He hears and sees! They have no guardian besides Him, and He shares His command with none.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command to Announce the Limit of One's Own Knowledge**

The verse opens with an instruction to speak, and what the Prophet ﷺ is told to say is that he does not know. God knows best how long they stayed — a declaration of superior knowledge that belongs to someone else.

The form is unusual for a messenger in the middle of delivering an account he has just been given. He has recited the story, supplied the number of years, and is now ordered to place that number beneath a higher knowledge. Nothing in the sequence suggests the figure was wrong; the ordering simply fixes who holds the information.

There is a polemical edge as well. The men who tested him wanted to measure his knowledge against another tradition's. The reply declares that on this subject the measure belongs to God alone, and no human tradition can claim it.

The instruction also models something for everyone who is asked a question he cannot answer. The honest reply is not a guess dressed as information, and the verse places that reply in the mouth of a prophet rather than leaving it as advice.

**The Unseen Has a Single Owner**

The second sentence generalises the point. With Him is the unseen of the heavens and the earth — not a portion of it, and not a shared custody.

The Qur'an repeats this assignment constantly: *“With Him are the keys of the unseen—no one knows them except Him”* (6:59). The formula is used to close questions rather than to open speculation, and it appears wherever someone claims knowledge of what has not been disclosed.

Applied here, it ends the argument about the cave. The number of the youths and the length of their stay belong to the category of undisclosed things, and the verse removes both from the range of what a person may assert with confidence.

The scope of the phrase is also total: the unseen of the heavens *and the earth*. Nothing in either realm is excluded, which rules out the common compromise of assigning God the heavens and leaving human enquiry the earth.

**"How Perfectly He Hears and Sees"**

The exclamation that follows shifts from knowledge to perception. The Arabic is an exclamation of quality — how well He hears, how well He sees — and it names the two faculties by which information reaches a knower.

The pairing is deliberate. Knowledge in the previous clause could be understood as pre-existing awareness; hearing and seeing describe God as attending to what occurs. He is not merely informed about events in advance but present to them as they happen.

The two clauses together answer an objection the story invites. If God placed the youths in a sealed cave and let centuries pass over them, was He attending? The verse answers with an exclamation about His hearing and sight rather than with an argument.

**No Guardian Besides Him**

The pronoun in *they have no guardian besides Him* is read in two ways. Al-Rāzī records both: it may refer to the youths of the cave, or to those who disputed about their affair.

The first reading fits the narrative. The young men entered a cave with no defender, no supplies, and no political protection, and the story's entire account of their preservation is an account of what God did for people who had nothing else.

The second fits the context of dispute. Those arguing about the number and the length have no authority to appeal to, and the verse tells them so.

Both readings are supported by the wording, and both are consistent with the Qur'an's use of *walī* elsewhere. The choice between them does not change the verse's force: whatever group is meant, there is no one else to turn to.

**No Partner in the Command**

The final clause concerns *ḥukm* — judgement, decision, command. God makes no one a partner in it, which the commentators explain in two directions.

The first is general: His judgement is final, with no appeal and no revision. The second is specific to this passage: what God has stated about the youths and their stay is the last word on the matter, and the disputants may not add to it.

The second reading gives the verse its closing function. A narrative that began with a challenge, moved through a story, and ended with a contested number is now sealed. The clause tells the reader that the account is complete.

It also excludes a practice the audience was accustomed to. Religious claims in that world were often settled by appeal to a priest, an ancestor, or a shrine. The verse states that on matters of divine judgement there is no such appeal, and no intermediary holding a share of the decision.

**How a Story Is Closed**

The ending is worth comparing with the way narratives usually end. There is no moral drawn, no lesson summarised, no exhortation attached. The cave account stops with four statements about God: what He knows, how He perceives, who has no guardian but Him, and who shares His command.

The shape tells the reader what the story was for. It was never about the youths as individuals; it was a demonstration of divine knowledge and power conducted through their case. When the demonstration is finished, the text returns to the subject it was demonstrating.

That is also why the sūrah moves on immediately to the Prophet's ﷺ standing instruction — to recite what has been revealed. The story has done its work, and the reciter's task resumes.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:27

> Recite what has been revealed to you from the Book of your Lord. None can change His Words, nor can you find any refuge besides Him

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Instruction to Recite, Not to Revise**

The command is short and its object is fixed: recite what has been revealed. The Prophet ﷺ is not asked to adapt the message, to soften a passage, or to produce a version suited to his audience.

The occasion explains the instruction. The Makkans had asked for exactly that — *“Bring us a different Quran or make some changes in it”* (10:15) — and the answer given there was that it is not his to change. This verse states the same ruling as a positive command rather than a refusal.

The placement after the cave story is significant. A long narrative has just been delivered, complete with details the audience wanted and details it did not. The instruction tells the reciter what to do with all of it: deliver it as received.
