The verb itself is worth holding. *Utlū* means to recite aloud, in sequence, to an audience. It is not a verb for private reading or for study. The task assigned is public delivery, which is why the demand to alter the text was a demand about what other people would hear.

**Words That Cannot Be Changed**

The second clause is absolute. None can change His Words — no person, no community, no generation. The Qur'an makes the same claim elsewhere in almost identical terms: *“The Word of your Lord has been perfected in truth and justice. None can change His Words”* (6:115).

The claim has a precise scope. It does not deny that God alters His own revelation, and the Qur'an says so plainly: *“When We replace a verse with another—and Allah knows best what He reveals—they say, ‘You ˹Muḥammad˺ are just a fabricator’”* (16:101). Replacement is a divine act; alteration by anyone else is excluded.

The distinction matters for how the text has been transmitted. A book whose words cannot be changed by its readers imposes a duty on them: preserve exactly, and argue with the meaning rather than with the wording.

The claim has been tested by history in a way few religious claims are. The Qur'an's text has been memorised in full by successive generations, and the claim that its words cannot be altered is falsifiable — anyone who can produce an early manuscript differing in wording can refute it. The claim stands because the memorisation stands.

**From the Book of Your Lord**

The genitive construction is doing quiet work. The revelation is described as coming *from the Book of your Lord* — a Book that belongs to God, of which what is recited is a portion.

The phrase answers the charge of authorship at its root. A book written by a man can be edited by him; a portion taken from someone else's book cannot. The Prophet ﷺ is placed in the position of a reader with access, not an author with control.

This is the same claim the sūrah opened with, when it praised God for sending the Book down to His servant. The opening stated it as praise; this verse states it as a restriction on what the servant may do.

The two statements belong together and neither works alone. A book sent down is not thereby unalterable by its bearer; a book declared unalterable is not thereby divine. The sūrah asserts both, and the second follows from the first.

**No Refuge Besides Him**

The final clause uses a word built on the root for inclining or turning aside — *multaḥad*, a place of deviation, a refuge one leans into. The negation says there is none besides God.

The word choice suits the context. A man asked to alter a revelation is being offered a way out: change the text, keep the audience, avoid the confrontation. The verse names that way out as an illusion. There is no shelter in compliance with the demand, because the One who sent the message is the only party whose judgement matters.

The clause also completes the verse's movement. Recite what was given; the words cannot be altered; and if you consider altering them, there is nowhere to go.

The same word appears in Sūrat al-Jinn, where those who turn away are told they will find no refuge from God. Its use here converts a threat into a statement of fact: the alternative to obedience is not shelter but exposure.

**Recitation as the Standing Commission**

The verse generalises beyond its occasion. The command to recite what has been revealed is the definition of the messenger's task, and the Qur'an states it in the same terms elsewhere: recite what has been revealed to you from the Book (29:45).

The commission excludes three things at once. It excludes invention, since only what was revealed may be recited. It excludes selection, since the object is the whole of what was revealed. And it excludes editorial judgement about audience, since the reciter's relation to the text is fixed by the verb.

What remains is a task that looks modest and is not: deliver the words, in order, without addition, to people who may not want them.

**The Same Refusal in Sūrat Yūnus**

The parallel passage supplies the reasoning this verse compresses: *“Say ˹to them˺, ‘It is not for me to change it on my own. I only follow what is revealed to me. I fear, if I were to disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day.’”* (10:15).

Sūrat Yūnus gives the motive — fear of the Day — where Sūrat al-Kahf gives the command. Together they describe a messenger who cannot change the text even if he wished to, and who does not wish to because he knows what compliance would cost.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:28

> And patiently stick with those who call upon their Lord morning and evening, seeking His pleasure. Do not let your eyes look beyond them, desiring the luxuries of this worldly life. And do not obey those whose hearts We have made heedless of Our remembrance, who follow ˹only˺ their desires and whose state is ˹total˺ loss

**Expanded Commentary**

**Patience Directed at Company, Not Only at Hardship**

The verb is unusual in its construction: keep *yourself* patient *with* those who call upon their Lord. The object of the patience is the speaker's own soul, and the preposition places that soul in the company of a specific group.

Most Qur'anic commands to patience concern endurance under hardship. This one concerns staying put among people. The difficulty being addressed is not persecution but the temptation to prefer better company.

The reflexive object sharpens it. The soul must be held in place, which implies that it wants to move. Nobody needs to be told to keep himself with people he already prefers.

The command therefore assumes a real temptation. It is addressed to a man whose followers were poor, persecuted, and socially worthless to the men who might otherwise have joined him. The instruction is not a courtesy but a demand that he resist an advantage.

The occasion is recorded in the exegetical tradition. Prominent Makkans indicated that they were willing to sit and listen to the Prophet ﷺ, on one condition: that his poorer followers leave, so that they would not have to share a gathering with people of low standing.

The demand was not about doctrine. Nobody asked for the message to be changed at this point; the request concerned who was present while it was delivered. The social composition of the gathering was treated as an obstacle to the notables' participation.

The Qur'an answers by forbidding the accommodation. The verse does not permit a separate session for the wealthy, a private audience, or a temporary exclusion. It commands the opposite: stay with the people they wanted removed.

**Morning and Evening**

The group is described by its practice: those who call upon their Lord morning and evening. The two times mark the structure of a devotional day — invocation at the start and at the close, with the hours between governed by them.

The commentators read the phrase both ways: literally, at those two times, and idiomatically, constantly. The two readings are not in competition, since a practice performed at both ends of the day shapes the whole of it.

The same formula appears as an instruction elsewhere: glorify Him morning and evening (33:42). The description here is therefore not a compliment but an identification — these are the people whose lives are organised around remembrance, and that is what makes them worth staying with.

The qualifier is decisive. They call upon their Lord *seeking His pleasure* — literally, desiring His Face. The phrase excludes every other motive: not status, not protection, not the benefits of belonging.

The Qur'an uses the same phrase for giving: *“We feed you only for the sake of Allah, seeking neither reward nor thanks from you”* (76:9). The motive is what distinguishes an act of worship from a social performance, and the verse identifies the group by it.

The mystics read the phrase at its furthest reach, as the desire for the vision of God itself. Whether read minimally or maximally, it fixes the direction of the group's attention — toward God rather than toward the gathering's other members.

**Eyes That Look Past Them**

The second command concerns the Prophet's ﷺ gaze. Do not let your eyes look beyond them, desiring the luxuries of this worldly life. The verb describes a gaze that travels past one group toward another.

The connection to the sūrah's opening is exact. The earth's adornment was described earlier as the instrument of a test, and this verse shows the test operating on the messenger himself — not through wealth offered to him but through the social value of wealthy followers.

The prohibition is not against noticing the wealthy. It is against looking past the sincere toward them, which is a specific movement of attention with a specific consequence: the people one looks past are being told what they are worth.

The final clause describes those whose instruction must be refused: hearts made heedless of God's remembrance, following their own desires, whose affair exceeds the bounds.

The three descriptors are ordered. Heedlessness of remembrance is the condition; following desire is its operation; excess is its result. A heart unattended by remembrance has nothing to check what it wants.

The passive construction is worth weighing. Their hearts were *made* heedless, which places the condition inside the divine ordering rather than treating it as a purely self-generated state. The Qur'an describes this repeatedly, and it always pairs the description with the person's own prior turning away.

**The Same Command in Sūrat al-Anʿām**

The parallel verse is more explicit about the stakes: *“˹O Prophet!˺ Do not dismiss those ˹poor believers˺ who invoke their Lord morning and evening, seeking His pleasure. You are not accountable for them whatsoever, nor are they accountable for you. So do not dismiss them, or you will be one of the wrongdoers”* (6:52).

The addition is the warning at the end. Dismissing the poor is not a social misjudgement but an act that makes the dismiss-er a wrongdoer. Sūrat al-Kahf states the command; Sūrat al-Anʿām states the consequence of ignoring it.

A third passage supplies the same lesson from the other direction. The Prophet ﷺ was reproved for frowning and turning away *“˹simply˺ because the blind man came to him ˹interrupting˺”* (80:1–2) — a momentary preference for the notables present, corrected in revelation before it hardened into a practice.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:29

> And say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “˹This is˺ the truth from your Lord. Whoever wills let them believe, and whoever wills let them disbelieve.” Surely We have prepared for the wrongdoers a Fire whose walls will ˹completely˺ surround them. When they cry for aid, they will be aided with water like molten metal, which will burn ˹their˺ faces. What a horrible drink! And what a terrible place to rest

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Truth Declared, Then Left Standing**

The instruction is to make a single declaration: this is the truth from your Lord. No argument accompanies it, no evidence is summarised, no appeal to the audience's interests is attached.

The declaration also fixes the source. The truth is from your Lord — not from the messenger, not from a tradition, not from a consensus. The possessive addresses the hearers directly, which makes the statement harder to treat as one party's claim against another's.

What follows is the messenger's discharge. He has been given a message and told to state it plainly; the rest belongs to those who hear it. The verse defines the limit of the prophetic task in one sentence.

The plainness is also strategic. A message offered with qualifications invites negotiation; a message offered as a bare statement of fact can only be accepted or rejected. The verse removes the middle ground on which the Makkans had been trying to conduct the discussion.

**"Whoever Wills" as a Warning, Not a Permission**

The next clause reads, taken alone, like an indifference: whoever wills let them believe, and whoever wills let them disbelieve. Read that way it would license unbelief, which is not what the verse means.

The second half settles it. The Fire is described immediately, in detail, as prepared for the wrongdoers. A permission followed by a description of the penalty is not a permission; it is a statement that the choice is real and that it has consequences.

The rhetorical structure is common in the Qur'an. The freedom announced is the freedom of a person standing at a fork, not of a person told the road does not matter. The verse removes the messenger from the decision without removing the weight of the decision.

**A Fire With Walls Around It**

The Fire is described as having a *surādiq* — a wall, canopy, or enclosing covering — that surrounds its inhabitants completely. The image is of an enclosure with no gap, not of a pit one might climb out of.

The Qur'an uses the same picture elsewhere: *“on the Day the punishment will overwhelm them from above them and from below their feet”* (29:55), and *“They will have layers of fire above and below them”* (39:16). The consistency of the image suggests it is meant literally rather than as a figure for intensity.

The detail answers an assumption people bring to descriptions of punishment: that there is an edge, a boundary, somewhere to retreat. The verse removes the edge.

The word itself is telling. A *surādiq* is what surrounds a tent or a pavilion — an enclosure made for those inside it. Applied to the Fire, it turns a place of punishment into a dwelling with no exit, which is why the verse ends by calling it a place to rest.

**Relief That Turns Out to Be Punishment**

The cruelest image in the verse is grammatical. When they cry for aid, they will be aided — the same verb in both clauses — with water like molten metal. The form of an answer to a plea is preserved while its content is inverted.

The substance is *muhl*, described by the commentators as molten metal, or as a thick liquid like the dregs of oil. It is offered in response to a request for water, and it burns the faces of those who approach it.

The verse then comments on both aspects at once: what a horrible drink, and what a terrible place to rest. The first concerns the substance; the second concerns the situation of anyone for whom such a substance counts as relief.

**Faces as the Whole Person**

The burning is located on the faces, and the Qur'an's usage explains why. Punishment and reward in the Hereafter are repeatedly described in terms of faces: faces darkened, faces bright, faces that day covered in dust.

The face stands for the person. It is what faces other people, what carries identity, and what is turned toward whatever one attends to. A punishment that reaches the face reaches the whole of the one who bears it.

The choice is also appropriate to the sin. Those described here turned their faces away from the truth when it was declared to them; the description of what meets their faces in the Fire is the counterpart of that turning.

The idiom also explains why the Qur'an speaks of faces on the Day of Judgment so often. A person's relation to God is described as a direction the face takes, and the outcome of that direction is described in the same terms.

**The Drinks of Hell Against the Drinks of the Garden**

The contrast is drawn explicitly elsewhere in the Qur'an, and the two descriptions are meant to be read together. The Garden's provision is listed in full: *“rivers of fresh water, rivers of milk that never changes in taste, rivers of wine delicious to drink, and rivers of pure honey”* (47:15), and the same verse ends by asking whether its inhabitants can be compared to those *“left to drink boiling water that will tear apart their insides.”*

Both descriptions use the same category — drink — and invert it. What quenches in one place destroys in the other. The Qur'an's method here is not abstract warning but concrete reversal: take the most ordinary comfort and show what becomes of it on the other side of the choice this verse has just placed before its hearers.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:30

> As for those who believe and do good, We certainly never deny the reward of those who are best in deeds

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Negation Reinforced Twice**

The promise is built on a double emphasis. The particle of certainty comes first — We certainly — and the verb that follows is negative: We never deny, or more literally, We do not cause to be lost. Two devices are stacked on a single statement, and both are needed to say what the verse says.

The result is a promise about preservation rather than about generosity. The verse does not primarily announce a reward; it announces that a reward already earned will not be lost. The concern it answers is the fear of having one's effort annulled.

That fear is not imaginary. A person who has done good and then fallen into hardship, or who has been wronged before seeing any return, can reasonably ask whether the good counted. The verse answers the question in the strongest form Arabic allows.

The audience had a specific reason to ask. The early believers in Makkah had lost property, family standing, and in some cases their freedom, and none of it had yet produced a visible return. The promise is addressed to people in exactly that position.

**Belief and Deed as a Single Condition**

The two are joined as they are throughout the Qur'an: those who believe and do good. Neither term alone carries the promise, and the conjunction is not incidental.

Belief without action produces no visible difference in the world, and the Qur'an repeatedly treats action as the evidence of belief. Action without belief has no direction and no address; it may benefit people, but it is not offered to anyone.

The same pairing governs the sūrah's opening, where the good news was given to the believers who do good. Nothing has been added to the condition since; the verse here confirms it after twenty-eight verses of narrative and warning.

The pairing also excludes two comfortable positions. A believer who does nothing has not met the condition, and a doer of good who believes nothing has not met it either. The verse leaves no category in which one half substitutes for the other.

**Reward Preserved Rather Than Merely Granted**

The verb chosen is significant. *Iḍāʿah* means to waste, to squander, to let something be lost through neglect. Its negation says that God does not mislay what people have done.

The Qur'an uses the same formula repeatedly, and always in contexts where effort has been costly: *“Surely Allah never discounts the reward of the good-doers”* (9:120), spoken of those who suffered thirst and fatigue in God's cause, and *“And be patient! Certainly Allah does not discount the reward of the good-doers”* (11:115).

The pattern identifies the audience of the promise. It is addressed to people whose good deeds have not yet produced any return in this world — which, at the time this sūrah was revealed, described most of its hearers who believed.

The verb's ordinary use reinforces the point. To *aḍāʿa* something is to lose it carelessly, as one loses a possession left unattended. Its negation says that no act of obedience is left unattended by God, however small it was or however long ago it was performed.

**Īmān, Islām, and Iḥsān**

The verse's two terms belong to a three-part scheme the Prophet ﷺ set out himself. In the report known as the ḥadīth of Jibrīl — recorded by Muslim (8) and al-Bukhārī (50) — the angel asks in turn about *islām*, *īmān*, and *iḥsān*, and receives three distinct answers: the practices, the beliefs, and the state of worshipping as though one sees God.

The verse names the second and implies the third. Those who believe and do good are described in its second half as *those who are best in deeds* — the language of *aḥsan*, from which *iḥsān* is formed.

The connection is more than lexical. A deed is *aḥsan* when it is both sincere and correct, and sincerity at its highest is the awareness of being seen. The verse therefore holds all three categories in a single sentence.

The scheme also explains why the Qur'an never treats belief as sufficient on its own. In the ḥadīth of Jibrīl the three are asked about separately, and the Prophet ﷺ answers each on its own terms — belief without practice is not the second category, and practice without awareness is not the third.

**The Hinge Between the Fire and the Garden**

The verse sits between two descriptions. The one before it ends with the molten water and the terrible place of rest; the one after it opens with the Gardens of Eternity. This verse is the pivot, and it is deliberately undecorated.

Its plainness is functional. After the vivid terrors of the Fire and before the vivid pleasures of the Garden, the reader is given a plain statement of the rule that connects a life to its outcome: believe, do good, and nothing of it will be lost.

The placement also answers the audience of the previous verse. Those told that whoever wills may disbelieve are now told what the willing actually leads to, in both directions, with the mechanism stated in between.

**The Same Pairing in Sūrat al-Bayyinah**

The parallel is short and unequivocal: *“Indeed, those who believe and do good—they are the best of ˹all˺ beings”* (98:7). The same two terms, the same superlative, and no additional condition attached.

Read beside this verse, the parallel confirms that the pairing is not a rhetorical habit but a definition. What makes a human being best is not knowledge alone, lineage, or intensity of feeling, but belief accompanied by action — and the reward attached to that combination is described in both passages as secure.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:31

> It is they who will have the Gardens of Eternity, with rivers flowing under their feet. There they will be adorned with bracelets of gold, and wear green garments of fine silk and rich brocade, reclining there on ˹canopied˺ couches. What a marvellous reward! And what a fabulous place to rest

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gardens of Eternity Named by Their Title**

The reward is introduced with a proper name: *Jannāt ʿAdn*, the Gardens of Eternity. The Qur'an uses the title repeatedly — in Sūrat al-Tawbah, al-Raʿd, al-Naḥl, Maryam, Ṭāhā, Fāṭir, Ṣād, Ghāfir, and al-Bayyinah — and each use carries the permanence in the name itself.

The title answers the previous verse directly. Those whose reward will not be lost are now told what the reward is called, and its name is the guarantee. No further assurance about duration is needed once the place has been named.

The plural is also worth noting. Gardens, not a garden. The image is of an estate rather than a single enclosed space, which suits a reward described as beyond calculation.

The phrase describes rivers flowing beneath the inhabitants, and it is one of the most repeated images in the Qur'an — it appears in more than two dozen places, from the second sūrah to the last.

The image works on several levels at once. Water in a desert climate is the first requirement of life, and rivers beneath one's feet mean that the requirement is permanently satisfied without effort. The positioning also implies elevation: the inhabitants are above the water, looking down at it, which places them in the posture of owners rather than of people queuing at a well.

The repetition is not padding. Each occurrence reattaches the promise of the Garden to the single most concrete good the original audience understood.

**Bracelets, Silk, and Brocade**

The description turns to adornment, and it is specific: bracelets of gold, garments of fine silk — *sundus* — and rich brocade, *istabrāq*. The two textiles are named separately because they differ: the first is the fine fabric, the second the thick, ornate weave.

The commentators divide over how literally to take the list. Many treat the items as symbols of a state that cannot be described in the language of this world, and al-Rāzī offers a distinction worth keeping: the garments represent what the inhabitants earned by their deeds, while the bracelets represent an addition from God beyond their earning.

The Sufi readings go further, taking every luxury in the description as a sign of nearness to God rather than of material comfort. The literal and the symbolic readings have coexisted in the tradition without either displacing the other.

One feature of the list would have struck the first audience immediately. Gold and silk were prohibited to the men of that community. Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī reported the Prophet ﷺ saying: *“Allah has permitted silk and gold to the females of my Ummah, and has forbidden them to the males”* (Sunan an-Nasaʾī 5265; Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī 1720), and ʿAlī reported him holding silk in one hand and gold in the other and declaring both forbidden to the men of his community (Sunan Abī Dāwūd 4057).

The parallel with wine is exact. What is withheld in this world for a reason is restored in the next, stripped of the harm that justified the prohibition. The Qur'an's paradise is not an escape from its own law but the place where the law's restrictions no longer apply because their causes are gone.

The men addressed by this verse are therefore promised precisely what they may not wear.

**Reclining on Canopied Couches**

The posture completes the scene. The inhabitants are reclining — *muttakiʾīn* — on couches described as canopied or curtained, and the same posture appears throughout the Qur'an's descriptions of the Garden.

In a society where sitting on the ground was normal and rough wool was the common cloth, reclining on a raised, curtained couch was the posture of royalty. The commentators say so plainly: the description presents the inhabitants as kings.

The choice of posture also carries a moral note. Reclining is the position of someone with nothing left to do — no labour, no defence, no provision to arrange. After a life of effort, the reward includes the cessation of effort.

The description ends with two exclamations: what a marvellous reward, and what a fabulous place to rest. The narrator interrupts his own account to evaluate it, which is unusual in Qur'anic description.

The two exclamations address different things. The first concerns what is received; the second concerns where it is received. A reward can be excellent in a poor place, and a place can be splendid while its contents are meagre; the verse closes both gaps.

The word for rest — *mustaqarr* — is the same root used for a settled abode. After the restless movement of the preceding verses, the passage ends where its audience most wanted it to end: somewhere they can stop.

**The Same Garments in Sūrat al-Insān**

The parallel description supplies a detail this verse omits: *“The virtuous will be ˹dressed˺ in garments of fine green silk and rich brocade, and adorned with bracelets of silver, and their Lord will give them a purifying drink”* (76:21).

Gold in one passage, silver in the other; the same garments in both. The variation has been read as indicating ranks within the Garden, and the difference in metal is the kind of detail the Qur'an varies when it describes states of unequal nearness.

What both passages share is the green. The colour appears here, in Sūrat al-Insān, and in the description of green cushions in Sūrat al-Raḥmān (55:76) — a consistent palette for the place this verse has just named.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:32

> Give them ˹O Prophet˺ an example of two men. To ˹the disbelieving˺ one We gave two gardens of grapevines, which We surrounded with palm trees and placed ˹various˺ crops in between

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Parable Set Against a Social Demand**

The verse opens with an instruction to tell a story, and its placement explains why. The passage before it has just forbidden the Prophet ﷺ to look past his poor followers toward the wealthy, and this verse supplies the argument that command needs.

A prohibition tells the hearer what not to do. A parable shows him what the alternative amounts to. The wealthy men whose company was preferred are now presented in narrative form, and the reader watches one of them arrive at a conclusion about his own wealth.

The transition is abrupt by design. From a command about where to look, the sūrah moves to a story about what is worth looking at, and the two are meant to be read as one movement.

**Two Men, Neither of Them Named**

The parable introduces two men and gives neither a name, a city, or a date. The commentators have supplied candidates — two brothers among the Israelites, or two of the Makhzūm clan in Makkah, one of whom spent his inheritance on property and the other on charity — but the text itself declines to identify them.

The anonymity is functional. A named man in a named city invites the hearer to treat the story as a case about someone else. Two unnamed men in an unspecified place make the story about a type, and every hearer can locate himself in one of the two positions.

The Qur'an's parables generally work this way. What matters is the relationship between the two figures, not their biography, and the relationship here is stated in the first clause: two men, one of them given more than the other.

**The Wealth Is Described as Given**

The grammar of the verse settles a question before it can be asked. *To one of them We gave two gardens* — the subject of the giving is God, and the object is the wealth. Nothing in the sentence attributes the gardens to the owner's effort, inheritance, or skill.

The point will matter later. When the owner boasts about what he has, the reader has already been told where it came from, and the boast becomes a claim about property whose title deed belongs to someone else.

The same construction governs the rest of the description: We surrounded them, We placed. Every element of the estate is attributed to the same agent, and the owner appears only as the recipient.

Note also that the other man is not described as deprived. The verse says what one was given and leaves the other's condition unstated, which is why the parable's contrast is about belief rather than about a complaint over distribution.

**A Garden Designed, Not Grown**

The layout is described with agricultural precision. Two gardens of grapevines; a border of date palms around them; crops planted between the rows. This is mixed cultivation — vines for fruit, palms for structure and shade, ground crops for the intervening soil.

The detail is not decorative. A reader who farms recognises the description as a productive, well-managed estate rather than a fantasy of abundance. The parable's force depends on the wealth being real and sensibly arranged.

The layout also has a defensive logic. Palms around the vines break the wind and reduce evaporation, and ground crops between the rows use soil that would otherwise lie idle. The description is of someone who has thought about the property, which makes the coming argument about gratitude harder to dismiss as a critique of incompetence.

The Qur'an's descriptions of gardens elsewhere serve the same function. Grapevines and date palms are its standard emblems of both worldly prosperity and divine favour, which is why they appear here as the content of a gift.

**The Makkans' Own Demand, Granted in Fiction**

The parable describes almost exactly what the Makkans had demanded as a condition of belief. They had said they would not believe *“until you have a garden of palm trees and vineyards, and cause rivers to flow abundantly in it”* (17:91).

The correspondence is too close to be accidental. The sūrah assembles the requested property — vines, palms, and, in the verse that follows, a river — and hands it to a man who does not believe. The demand is met in the form of a story, and the outcome shows what the property produces in its owner.

The argument is compact: the thing you made a condition of faith is here, and here is what it does to a person who has it.

The reply is also a refusal to meet the demand on its own terms. No garden was produced for the Makkans to inspect. A garden was described in a story, and the description was turned back on them as a question about what property does to the people who hold it.

**The Owners of the Garden in Sūrat al-Qalam**

The Qur'an offers a second parable of the same shape, and the comparison sharpens this one. *“Indeed, We have tested those ˹Meccans˺ as We tested the owners of the garden—when they swore they would surely harvest ˹all˺ its fruit in the early morning”* (68:17).

In Sūrat al-Qalam the owners are a group who exclude the poor from their harvest and lose the garden overnight. Here the owner is a single man who loses it after a conversation. Both parables attach the loss to a claim of ownership, and both address the same audience.

The pairing establishes a rule in the Qur'an's parables: property is a test of what its holder believes about where it came from.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:33

> Each garden yielded ˹all˺ its produce, never falling short. And We caused a river to flow between them

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Harvest That Never Failed**

The two gardens are described in a single clause: each brought its produce and did not fall short in any respect. The Arabic idiom behind the translation is stronger than the English — literally, the gardens *did him no wrong*.

That phrasing is deliberate and it will be echoed. A garden that fails a season wrongs its owner; these did not. Whatever goes wrong in the parable, the verse states at the outset that the gardens are not the cause of it.

The absence of failure is also what makes the owner's later confidence intelligible. A man whose property has never disappointed him has empirical grounds for expecting it not to. The parable is careful to make his error a reasonable one rather than an absurd one.

The phrase *never falling short* also excludes the ordinary explanations for a bad year. No drought, no blight, no poor season is left available as an excuse, and no credit can be claimed by the owner for managing risk well. The gardens simply did not fail, and the verse declines to say why.

**The River That Completed the Gift**

The second clause adds the one element an Arabian estate cannot produce for itself: water. We caused a river to flow between them — a perennial stream running through the middle of the two gardens.

The addition is decisive. Vines and palms in that climate are entirely dependent on irrigation, and a property with its own river is immune to the drought that ends most such estates. The gift is therefore not merely generous but secured against the ordinary risk.

It also completes the echo of the Makkans' demand, which had asked for a garden of palms and vines with rivers caused to flow in the midst of it (17:91). Every item on their list is now present, and the man who holds it does not believe.

The river is placed *between* the two gardens rather than beside them, which means both properties share it. Water in that climate is the one asset that cannot be duplicated, and its position makes the estate a single holding rather than two separate ones.

**Every Element Attributed to the Same Giver**

The grammar of the two verses is consistent. We made two gardens, We surrounded them, We placed crops between them, We caused a river to flow. Four divine acts, and the owner appears in none of them as an agent.

The pattern is not incidental to the parable; it is the parable's argument in its syntax. By the time the owner speaks, the reader has been told four times where the property came from.

That is why his boast lands as a claim of theft. He is not exaggerating what he has; he is misdescribing its source, and the verses have already supplied the correct description twice over.

The method is worth naming, because the Qur'an uses it constantly. Rather than arguing with a false claim, it states the true one first and lets the reader hear the falsehood against it. No refutation is needed once the correct account is already on the page.

**The Description Does Not Caricature Wealth**

Nothing in these two verses criticises the gardens. They are productive, well-designed, watered, and reliable, and the text says so without reservation.

The honesty matters for the parable's force. A story in which the rich man's property is shoddy or his success fraudulent would teach only that dishonest wealth fails. This story teaches something harder: wealth that is real, well-managed, and honestly held can still produce a false conclusion about its owner's position.

The Qur'an's parables consistently work this way. The thing condemned is never the possession but the inference drawn from it, and the inference is only tempting because the possession is genuine.

**What the Reader Is Meant to Want**

The description is attractive on purpose. Anyone who has farmed in a dry climate reads these two verses as a description of the best outcome available — a property that produces every year and has its own water.

The parable needs that attraction. If the reader did not want the gardens, the owner's confidence would be incomprehensible and the warning inert. The sūrah builds the temptation in two verses before it examines what the temptation does to a mind.

This is the same mechanism described at the start of the sūrah, where the earth's adornment was made for the purpose of testing which of its inhabitants is best in deeds. The gardens are that adornment in a single estate.

The parable is therefore the sūrah's own seventh verse worked out as a story. What was stated as a principle — adornment given in order to test — is here given a name, a river, and an owner, so that the reader can watch the test being taken and failed.

**Adornment That Has an Ending**

The verse leaves its description unqualified, and the qualification comes from elsewhere in the Qur'an. The inventory of worldly good ends with a verdict: *“These are the pleasures of this worldly life, but with Allah is the finest destination”* (3:14).

Applied here, the verdict does not disparage the gardens. It locates them. A property that never fails is still a property of this world, and the parable's task is to show what happens when a man treats that as the whole description of what he owns.

The limitation is temporal rather than moral. The gardens are good, and a reader who wants them has understood the description correctly. What the Qur'an objects to is the word *never* — a term no earthly property can honestly carry.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:34

> And he had other resources ˹as well˺. So he boasted to a ˹poor˺ companion of his, while conversing with him, “I am greater than you in wealth and superior in manpower.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wealth Beyond the Two Gardens**

The verse begins by widening the inventory. Beyond the gardens he had *thamar*, a word that means fruit but also profit, produce, and accumulated wealth of every kind. The commentators read it both ways: the harvest of the gardens, and resources of other sorts including gold and silver.

The widening matters. The parable has described a landed estate; this clause adds liquid wealth to it. The man is not merely a farmer with good soil but a holder of capital, which is a different social position and a different kind of security.

The effect on the argument is cumulative. Two gardens that never fail, a river of his own, and reserves beyond both — by the time he speaks, the reader understands why he speaks that way.

The double meaning of the word is also useful to the parable. Fruit is what a garden gives each season; profit is what a man accumulates. A single term covering both quietly links the estate to the wealth, and the boast that follows draws on both at once.

**A Boast Delivered in Conversation**

The setting is specified with care: he boasted to his companion *while conversing with him*. This is not a soliloquy, a proclamation, or a speech to a crowd. It is a remark made in the course of a conversation between two men who know each other.

The detail makes the boast more revealing rather than less. Public bragging performs for an audience; a boast made privately to a companion discloses what the speaker actually thinks when he is not managing an impression.

The phrase *while conversing with him* is repeated in the verse that records the companion's reply, which frames the exchange as a single dialogue. The parable is structured as a debate between two positions, not as a portrait of one man.

That structure determines how the reader is meant to take it. The rich man is not condemned by the narrator; he is answered by another character. The judgement arrives inside the story rather than from outside it, which makes it harder to dismiss as editorial.

**The Two Measures of Standing**

The boast names two things and no others: wealth and manpower. The pair is the standard measure of status in the Qur'an's descriptions of worldly success, and the two together cover both property and people.

The Book warns repeatedly that the pair proves nothing about divine favour. *“Adding, ‘We are far superior ˹to the believers˺ in wealth and children, and we will never be punished’”* (34:35) is answered with the plain statement that wealth and children are not a sign of standing with God.

The man in the parable uses the two measures exactly as Sūrat Sabaʾ describes. He is not an eccentric; he is the ordinary case, stating the ordinary assumption of his society in the ordinary terms.

**A Comparison That Reveals the Speaker**

The boast is comparative, and the comparison is the point. *I am greater than you* requires a lesser person to be present, and the man has chosen his audience accordingly.

Nobody boasts in these terms to a superior or to an equal. The remark is addressed to the one person in his circle whose poverty makes the comparison available, which says more about the speaker than the statement itself does.

The parable is precise about who that person is: a companion. Not a servant, not a stranger, but someone whose company he keeps. The boast is delivered inside a friendship, and its content denies the equality that friendship assumes.

**Manpower as a Claim to Security**

The second measure deserves its own attention. *Stronger in men* means sons, retainers, servants, and dependants — the human force that in a tribal society stood between a man and his enemies.

The claim is therefore about more than prestige. It is about protection. A man with many dependants cannot be attacked with impunity, cannot be cheated, and cannot be ignored, and the boast is announcing that he has arrived at that position.

There is also a tribal assumption embedded in it. Manpower is counted in sons first and retainers second, and both are understood as continuing after the father. The boast is thus about the future as much as the present — a lineage, not merely a household.

The parable will test exactly this claim. When the gardens are destroyed, the verse that follows the loss states that he had no manpower to help him and could not help himself. The two statements are meant to be read against each other.

The test is severe because the claim was specific. He did not say he was respected or admired; he said he was *stronger in men*, which is a claim about capacity in a crisis. The crisis arrives, and the capacity proves to have been an inference from prosperity rather than a fact about his dependants.

**The Same Boast in Sūrat Sabaʾ**

The parallel is exact in substance and tone: *“Adding, ‘We are far superior ˹to the believers˺ in wealth and children, and we will never be punished.’”* (34:35).

Sūrat Sabaʾ supplies what Sūrat al-Kahf leaves to the narrative: the inference drawn from the comparison. The men there conclude from their wealth that punishment will not reach them, which is the same movement the garden owner makes two verses later when he says the Hour will not come.

The two passages together identify a pattern the Qur'an treats as diagnostic. Wealth and children are read as a verdict from God, and the verdict is then used to argue that no further reckoning can be expected.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:35

> And he entered his property, while wronging his soul, saying, “I do not think this will ever perish

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wrong Is Named Before the Words Are Quoted**

The verse arranges its elements in a specific order: he entered his garden, *wronging himself*, and said. The moral verdict is inserted before the speech it judges, so the reader meets the statement already classified.

That arrangement prevents a sympathetic reading. A man entering his property and remarking on its permanence might otherwise sound like any owner taking satisfaction in his work. The phrase *ẓāliman li-nafsih* removes the ambiguity before the quotation begins.

The Qur'an uses the same formula throughout for sin: wronging oneself. The description is precise, since the damage described in the following verses falls on the owner rather than on anyone else.

The phrase also removes any suggestion of an injured third party. Nobody was cheated, no poor man was defrauded, no neighbour was harmed. The parable's sin is entirely internal, which is why its punishment is the loss of the thing that produced it.

**Entering as an Act of Ownership**

The verb is ordinary — he entered — but the possessive attached to the garden is not. He enters *his* garden, and the possessive is the verse's first claim under examination.

The two preceding verses attributed every element of the property to God. This verse records the owner's own attribution, and the difference between them is the whole subject of the parable. Nothing in his words denies God's existence; what they do is relocate the property.

That is why the sin is called association rather than denial. The owner is not an atheist in the modern sense. He is a man who has quietly installed himself alongside the giver as a second source, and the parable treats that as the gravest available error.

The physical act matters too. He goes into the garden to look at it, and what he says is what the sight produces. The parable locates the error in an act of inspection rather than in an argument.

**"I Do Not Think This Will Ever Perish"**

The statement is a prediction about permanence, and it is stated with the confidence of a man reporting what he has observed. His gardens have never failed; therefore they will not.

The inference is where the wrong lies. Regularity in the past does not establish necessity in the future, and the Qur'an states the alternative in the plainest terms: *“Everything is bound to perish except He Himself”* (28:88).

The claim is also a quiet assertion of independence. A thing that cannot perish does not depend on anyone to sustain it, and the owner has just described his property in terms that release it from that dependence.

The statement is worth comparing with the sūrah's earlier warning about the earth itself. What is on it will be reduced to barren ground, and the warning is universal. The owner has exempted one plot of land from a rule the Qur'an applies to the planet.

**The Long Hope**

The commentators name the condition precisely: *ṭūl al-amal*, the lengthening of hope. It is the state in which a person's expectations extend indefinitely forward, and in which death stops functioning as a limit on planning.

The Qur'an identifies the same condition elsewhere and mocks it: *“thinking that their wealth will make them immortal!”* (104:3). The garden owner does not quite say that, but the distance between his statement and that one is small — a property that never perishes implies an owner who does not need to leave it.

The diagnosis explains why the parable is about a rich man rather than a wicked one. The error is not greed; it is an error about time, produced by the very reliability of his possessions.

**Why Abundance Produces the Delusion**

The mechanism is worth setting out, because the parable depends on it. A man whose property fails occasionally is reminded that it is contingent. A man whose property never fails receives no such reminder, and the absence of reminders hardens into a theory.

The sūrah described this at its opening. The earth's adornment was made in order to test which of its inhabitants is best in deeds, and the test operates precisely through reliability — a world that disappointed regularly would test no one, because nobody would trust it enough to be attached.

The garden owner is failing the test described in the seventh verse. He has taken the adornment for the destination, and the verse that follows shows where that reading leads.

The parable is therefore a case study rather than a fable about a bad man. Its subject is a competent owner of good property who drew the wrong conclusion from it, and the reader is meant to recognise how easy the conclusion was to draw.

**Everything Perishes Except His Face**

The corrective is stated elsewhere without qualification: *“And do not invoke any other god with Allah. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. Everything is bound to perish except He Himself. All authority belongs to Him. And to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned”* (28:88).

The verse pairs the perishing of all things with the return of all people to God, which is exactly the connection the garden owner refused to make. He separated his property from the general rule of endings, and in doing so separated himself from the reckoning that follows those endings.

Sūrat al-Raḥmān states the same fact in a single line: *“Every being on earth is bound to perish”* (55:26). Read beside the garden owner's claim, the two verses measure the distance between what a man sees from inside his property and what is true of it.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:36

> nor do I think the Hour will ˹ever˺ come. And if in fact I am returned to my Lord, I will definitely get a far better outcome than ˹all˺ this.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**From a Permanent Garden to a Cancelled Day**

The second statement follows the first as a consequence. A man who has concluded that his property will never perish has already removed the event that would end it, and the verse records the next step: the Hour itself is dismissed.

The movement is not a logical leap so much as a settling. If this estate is permanent, then nothing interrupts it, and the Day of Judgment is the largest possible interruption. Denying the second is the natural completion of asserting the first.

The Qur'an frequently observes this sequence in reverse as well — belief in the Hour produces detachment from property. The connection between eschatology and economics runs in both directions, and the parable is showing one direction in real time.

The two denials also support each other. If the Hour will not come, permanence is possible; if permanence is observable in one's own estate, the Hour becomes unnecessary. Each belief supplies evidence for the other, which is why neither can be corrected on its own.

**A Conditional That Conceals a Denial**

The hedge deserves attention: *if in fact I am returned to my Lord*. On the surface this is an admission that the Hour might come. In context it functions as the opposite.

The conditional allows the speaker to deny the premise while arguing about its consequences. He does not believe he will be returned; he is describing what would happen if he were, and the description is a boast rather than a hope.

The construction is common in argument between unequals. Conceding a premise hypothetically costs nothing and permits the speaker to continue boasting inside a framework he has just refused to accept.

The Qur'an identifies the move by name in Sūrat Maryam, asking whether the man has looked into the unseen or taken a pledge from God (19:78). Neither has happened, which leaves the conditional without content — a boast wearing the grammar of humility.

**Merit Assumed to Outlive Death**

The reasoning behind the boast is recoverable, and al-Rāzī states it: the man believes his wealth belongs to him on the basis of his own merit, and since merit does not die with the body, he expects to be provided for after death on the same terms.

The inference has a shape worth naming. Present favour is read as a verdict on the person; a verdict on the person survives the person; therefore further favour can be expected. Every step is questionable, and the Qur'an attacks the first step wherever it appears.

The same claim is recorded elsewhere with the same confidence: *“Have you seen ˹O Prophet˺ the one who rejects Our revelations yet boasts, ‘I will definitely be granted ˹plenty of˺ wealth and children ˹if there is an afterlife˺.’?”* (19:77), followed immediately by the question that dismantles it: *“Has he looked into the unseen or taken a pledge from the Most Compassionate?”* (19:78).

**The Inference the Qur'an Attacks Everywhere**

Reading prosperity as approval is one of the Book's standing targets. The mechanism has a name in the tradition — *istidrāj*, the gradual drawing-on of a person who mistakes increase for endorsement — and the Qur'an describes it without softening: *“When they became oblivious to warnings, We showered them with everything they desired. But just as they became prideful of what they were given, We seized them by surprise”* (6:44).

The same theme runs through Sūrat al-Qalam: *“So leave to Me ˹O Prophet˺ those who reject this message. We will gradually draw them to destruction in ways they cannot comprehend. I ˹only˺ delay their end for a while, but My planning is flawless”* (68:44–45).

Qārūn states the premise openly and is answered with a history lesson: *“He replied, ‘I have been granted all this because of some knowledge I have.’ Did he not know that Allah had already destroyed some from the generations before him who were far superior to him in power?”* (28:78). The garden owner makes the same claim in different words.

**"Better Than This" Uses the Same Measure**

The boast is comparative once more. He does not hope for forgiveness, for nearness, or for peace; he expects something *better than this*, which means better in the units he already uses.

That detail is the most revealing in the verse. A man whose idea of the Hereafter is a larger estate has not changed his scale of value, only his horizon. The Qur'an's descriptions of the Garden use different categories entirely, and the difference is the point.

The verse therefore indicts the boast twice: for its confidence and for its imagination. Even if the man were right about the Hour, he would be wrong about what he would find there.

**Almost the Same Words in Sūrat Fuṣṣilat**

The parallel is so close that it reads as the same statement in another register: *“And if We let them taste a mercy from Us after being touched with adversity, they will certainly say, ‘This is what I deserve. I do not think the Hour will ˹ever˺ come. And if in fact I am returned to my Lord, the finest reward with Him will definitely be mine.’”* (41:50).

Sūrat Fuṣṣilat adds the first clause that Sūrat al-Kahf leaves implicit — *this is what I deserve* — and supplies the answer the parable will deliver narratively: We will inform the disbelievers of what they used to do.

The two passages together show that the garden owner is not an individual case but a type the Qur'an returns to whenever it examines what prosperity does to belief.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:37

> His ˹believing˺ companion replied, while conversing with him, “Do you disbelieve in the One Who created you from dust, then ˹developed you˺ from a sperm-drop, then formed you into a man

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Reply That Opens as a Question**

The companion's answer does not begin with a denial, a rebuke, or a counter-claim. It begins with a question: do you disbelieve in the One who created you?

The interrogative form is a rhetorical choice with a purpose. A statement can be contradicted; a question puts the burden of answering on the person addressed. The owner has been asked to account for a position he has never examined.

The verb also fixes the character of the offence. Disbelief here is not doubt or hesitation but *kufr* — a refusal of something known. The question implies that the matter is not genuinely open, which is why it is posed as a question rather than argued as a case.

The address is also personal throughout. Not *do you disbelieve in God* but *in the One who created you*. The argument is aimed at this man's own history rather than at a general proposition, which is why it cannot be deflected into theology.

**Three Stages, Named in Order**

The question lists an origin in three movements: created from dust, developed from a sperm-drop, formed as a man. The sequence is the Qur'an's standard account of human origin, and it appears in the same order elsewhere.

Sūrat al-Ḥajj sets it out at length: *“We did create you from dust, then from a sperm-drop, then ˹developed you into˺ a clinging clot ˹of blood˺, then a lump of flesh”* (22:5). Sūrat al-Muʾminūn continues through the stages to the finished creature (23:14), and Sūrat al-Qiyāmah compresses the whole account into a single line: *“Were they not ˹once˺ a sperm-drop emitted?”* (75:37).

The two endpoints do the work. Dust is the lowest material available; a formed man is a moral agent. Between them lies nothing the man himself contributed.

The middle term matters too. The sperm-drop is the stage at which a person is least distinguishable from any other, and naming it removes the possibility of claiming distinction by birth. Whatever the owner's family standing, his origin was identical to the poorest man's in the city.

**Why Origin Is the Answer to a Boast**

The choice of argument matches the boast exactly. The owner claimed superiority in wealth and manpower; the reply names the substance he is made of.

The contrast is not accidental but designed. Wealth is accumulated; origin is given. A man measured by what he has gathered is reminded of what he was before he gathered anything, and the reminder is not an insult but a correction of the scale.

The Qur'an uses this argument repeatedly because it is available to everyone. No learning is required to know where one came from, and no opponent can dispute it without disputing his own existence.

That availability is not a weakness of the argument but its strength. A proof requiring expertise can be dismissed by those without it; this one cannot be evaded by wealth, position, or education. The poor companion's disadvantage in the parable is social, and the argument he chooses is one in which that disadvantage disappears.

**The Question Carries Its Own Answer**

A question of this form expects one answer, and the expected answer is no. Nobody who has followed the three stages can deny the agent who arranged them, which is why the question is put instead of the conclusion.

The commentators add a further step. Al-Rāzī notes that being *formed as a man* includes the endowment of intelligence and moral responsibility — *taklīf*. The third stage is therefore not only physical but normative: the same act that made him a man made him accountable.

That completes the argument. A creature who was nothing, was made into an accountable being, and now claims independence from the one who made him has contradicted the only facts about himself that he cannot dispute.

The structure of the reply is worth naming as a method. It does not add a premise to the other man's claim; it replaces the claim's foundation. Once the speaker's origin is on the table, the boast about wealth has nothing to stand on.

**The Man With Nothing Holds the Argument**

The parable's social arrangement is inverted at this point, and the inversion is part of its teaching. The wealthy man has property, dependants, and standing; the poor man has an argument.

The argument does not depend on anything the poor man lacks. It requires no scholarship, no access to scripture, and no authority — only the observation that both men were made of dust. The Qur'an has deliberately equipped the weaker party with the stronger position.

This is the same reversal the preceding passage commanded the Prophet ﷺ to respect. He had been told to stay with those who call on their Lord morning and evening rather than look past them toward the wealthy, and the parable now demonstrates why: the standing of the two groups is not what it appears.

**The Same Sequence in Sūrat al-Ḥajj**

The parallel argument is set out in full where the Qur'an addresses resurrection directly: *“O humanity! If you are in doubt about the Resurrection, then ˹know that˺ We did create you from dust, then from a sperm-drop, then ˹developed you into˺ a clinging clot ˹of blood˺, then a lump of flesh —fully formed or unformed—in order to demonstrate ˹Our power˺ to you”* (22:5).

Both passages use origin as evidence, and both move from the first stage to the last without intermediary agents. Sūrat al-Ḥajj draws the conclusion the garden owner refused: the one who assembled a person once can assemble him again.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:38

> But as for me: He is Allah, my Lord, and I will never associate anyone with my Lord ˹in worship˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Statement That Begins With Contrast**

The reply opens with a turn: *but as for me*. The companion does not continue the argument by adding premises. He states where he stands and leaves the comparison standing between the two positions.

The construction is common in Arabic disputation and is used here with precision. After a question the other man cannot answer, the speaker moves from interrogation to declaration, and the declaration is personal rather than general.

The personal form is also the harder one. A general statement about God's oneness can be made by anyone without cost; a statement beginning *as for me* commits the speaker and invites the reply that his position is the weaker of the two.

The shift also changes the register. The previous verse argued about what God did; this one reports what the speaker believes. Argument has done what it can, and what remains is a statement of position.

**Three Claims in a Single Sentence**

The declaration contains three distinct affirmations. He is Allah — the name that fixes who is meant. He is my Lord — the relation that fixes what that One is to the speaker. I will never associate anyone with my Lord — the commitment that follows from both.

The order is not arbitrary. Identity comes first, then relation, then consequence. A person who knows who God is, and knows that this God is his Lord, has already settled the question of worship before stating it.

The repetition of *my Lord* twice in one sentence is emphatic rather than redundant. The possessive is the operative word: the claim being made is not about God in general but about a relation the speaker holds personally, and the second use ties the refusal of partners directly to that relation.

The compactness is worth noting. Everything the parable's debate requires is contained in one sentence, and the sentence is available to anyone regardless of learning or property.

**Lordship as the Ground of Worship**

The word *rabb* carries more than the English "Lord". It denotes the one who owns, sustains, and brings something to completion — the one responsible for a thing from its beginning to its end.

That is why the word answers the boast so exactly. The owner has been describing himself as the source of his property; the companion uses for God the term that means source and sustainer. The disagreement between them is about who holds that position, and the word itself states the answer.

The same word grounds the argument of the previous verse. The one who created him from dust is his *rabb*, and a creature's relation to its sustainer is not optional or negotiable.

The word also answers the garden's ownership question in a single term. Whatever a man maintains, the one who sustained him from dust holds the prior claim, and the parable's dispute is settled by the vocabulary its characters use.

**A Refusal With No Expiry Date**

The negation is permanent: *I will never associate*. The particle used — *lan* — denies the possibility for all time rather than for the present moment.

The permanence is the substantive claim. A person who refuses to worship other gods today, because it is currently inconvenient or unfashionable, has not made the declaration this verse records. What is stated here is a disposition rather than a decision.

The wording also matches the declaration of the cave youths earlier in the sūrah, who said they would never call on any god besides God. The same formula, the same permanence, and in both cases spoken by people with nothing to protect.

Permanence is what distinguishes the confession from a mood. The parable's owner will lose his property within verses, and a faith stated for the present moment would have gone with it. A faith stated without an expiry does not.

**What the Confession Costs the Man Who Makes It**

The declaration is easy to say and expensive to hold. The speaker has no property, no dependants, and no social standing; his refusal to associate partners with God has already cost him the position that the other man is boasting about.

The commentators draw out the consequence. Al-Qushayrī and al-Rāzī both note that a person who knows wealth and poverty come from the same source is grateful in plenty and patient in adversity, because neither state appears to him as a verdict on his worth.

That is the parable's real contrast. The rich man's position depends on his property continuing; the poor man's position depends on nothing that can be taken from him.

The difference is not one of temperament but of foundation. Two men in the same conversation hold positions of unequal security, and the one with the weaker social standing has the stronger ground. That inversion is what the preceding verses commanded the Prophet ﷺ to respect.

**The Same Confession From the Youths of the Cave**

The parallel inside this sūrah is exact in structure: *“Our Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth. We will never call upon any god besides Him, or we would truly be uttering an outrageous lie”* (18:14).

Both declarations move from lordship to a permanent refusal, and both attach the same verdict to the alternative — associating a partner with God is a lie, not a mistake. The youths faced a king for saying it; the companion says it to a man who is offering him nothing.

The recurrence fixes the shape of the confession the sūrah treats as central. It names the Lord, states the relation, and refuses the alternative without a time limit.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:39

> If only you had said, upon entering your property, ‘This is what Allah has willed! There is no power except with Allah!’ Even though you see me inferior to you in wealth and offspring

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Should Have Been Said**

The companion identifies the omission precisely. On entering his garden, the owner should have said: *mā shāʾa Allāh, lā quwwata illā billāh* — this is what Allah has willed, and there is no power except with Allah.

The two clauses do different work. The first attributes what is seen to a will other than the owner's. The second denies that any power operates independently of that will. Together they restate the whole of the parable's theology in eight words.

The timing is specified too: *upon entering*. The formula is not a general creed to be recited occasionally but a response to a particular moment — the moment a person stands inside something impressive that belongs to him.

The specificity explains why the omission mattered. The owner did not fail a general duty of belief; he missed a single sentence at a single moment, and the whole of what follows comes from that miss. The parable measures the distance between one phrase and a ruined estate.

**What the Formula Would Have Changed**

Nothing about the gardens would have been different. The vines would still have fruited and the river would still have run. What the formula changes is the description, and the parable's whole argument is that the description is where the danger lies.

A man who enters his property saying *this is what Allah willed* has located the source correctly, and the location governs everything that follows. He can lose the property without losing his account of the world, because the account never depended on the property.

The formula is therefore protective rather than merely pious. It does not increase the estate or reduce it; it insulates the owner against the loss the estate will eventually suffer, which is the only real risk the parable identifies.

The owner's failure is now fully specified. He did not sin by enjoying his garden; he sinned by narrating it to himself in a way that made him its origin.

**No Power Except With God**

The second clause is one of the most repeated phrases in Islamic devotion, known as the *ḥawqalah*. It denies *ḥawl* — movement or change of state — and *quwwah* — the capacity to effect it — to anything apart from God.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the phrase a specific standing. Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī reported that the Prophet ﷺ heard him saying it and asked whether he should be told of a word that is one of the treasures of Paradise, then named this phrase (reported by al-Bukhārī and Muslim).

Its use here is not devotional decoration. The owner has just claimed strength in manpower and permanence in property; the clause denies both, and denies them in the abstract form that covers every future claim of the same kind.

**"Even Though You See Me Inferior"**

The concession is as important as the rebuke. The poor man does not dispute the comparison. He accepts that he is inferior in wealth and offspring, and he builds his reply on that acceptance.

The move is strategically decisive. A man who contests the measure can be answered with more figures. A man who grants the measure and then shows that it settles nothing has removed the ground of the argument entirely.

The wording also preserves the parable's honesty. Islam does not deny that some people have more than others; it denies that the difference measures standing with God. The companion's concession makes that distinction visible rather than asserted.

His concession also removes the last refuge available to the boaster. A man challenged on his figures will defend them; a man granted his figures has nothing left to argue about except what they mean.

**Advice Delivered After the Boast**

The order of the companion's speech is instructive. He began with a question about the man's origin, moved to his own confession of faith, and only now offers the correction about what should have been said.

The sequence matters pastorally. A rebuke delivered first would have ended the conversation; a question about origin opened it, and a personal confession removed any suggestion that the speaker was merely attacking. The advice arrives inside an established exchange rather than as an interruption.

The parable is therefore also a model of how to disagree with someone who is boastful — beginning with what both parties share, which in this case is dust.

**A Treasure of Paradise in the Ṣaḥīḥ**

The phrase the companion recommends has a documented place in the Prophet's ﷺ teaching. The report from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī is recorded in both Ṣaḥīḥ collections, and the description attached to it — a treasure of Paradise — places the *ḥawqalah* among the shortest and most highly valued of devotional formulas.

The connection to this verse is not incidental. The formula is recommended at the moment of admiration for one's own possessions, which is precisely the moment the garden owner mishandled. What the parable presents as a missed sentence, the ḥadīth literature presents as a standing practice.

Read together, the two texts turn a narrative detail into a discipline: the sentence the rich man should have said is the sentence believers are taught to say.

The practice is also economical. It requires no scholarship, no wealth, and no time, and it is available at the exact moment when a person is most likely to forget it — standing in something impressive that belongs to him. That is why the parable treats the omission as serious and the remedy as simple.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:40

> perhaps my Lord will grant me ˹something˺ better than your garden, and send down upon your garden a thunderbolt from the sky, turning it into a barren waste

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Hope Set Against the Boast**

The companion states what he expects, and the statement is framed as the exact counterpart of the owner's. The rich man said he would receive something better than his garden if returned to his Lord; the poor man says his Lord may give him something better than that garden.

The symmetry is deliberate. Both men are talking about a better outcome, and the parable invites the reader to compare the grounds of the two expectations. One rests on property already held; the other rests on nothing except the relation stated in the previous verse.

The verse thereby reverses the parable's social logic. The man with nothing is the one making a confident claim about the future, and he makes it without boasting.

The reversal is not presented as a consolation prize for the poor. The companion's hope is stated in the same terms as the owner's — something better than your garden — which means the parable is comparing two claims on their merits rather than reversing them by decree.

**The Word "Perhaps" as Theology**

The particle is *ʿasā* — perhaps, it may be. The companion does not promise himself a garden and does not claim to know what God will do.

The contrast with the owner's language is exact and instructive. The rich man used the particle of permanent negation about the Hour and an emphatic assertion about his own reward; the poor man uses a particle of possibility about a gift he has not received.

The two men therefore differ not in optimism but in epistemic honesty. The one who is certain has no grounds; the one who hopes has grounds and declines to convert them into certainty.

The distinction is the sūrah's own. It has just instructed the Prophet ﷺ never to say *I will definitely do this tomorrow* without adding *if Allah so wills*, and the poor companion is the only character in the parable who speaks in that manner. The owner speaks in the forbidden register; the believer speaks in the commanded one.

**A Reckoning Sent From the Sky**

The second half names a specific mechanism: a *ḥusbān* from the sky. The word is read in two directions by the commentators — a destructive bolt or shower, and a decree that has been calculated and sent.

Both readings suit the context. The first describes the event; the second describes its character as something measured rather than random. A calamity that is *ḥusbān* is not bad weather but an accounting.

The direction is also significant. The garden's water came from a river the owner counted as his own; the destruction comes from above, from a source he does not control. The parable answers one supply with another.

The reversal is exact in kind. Water made the gardens possible and was the element the owner could least do without; water sent from the sky in the wrong quantity destroys them. Nothing foreign is introduced into the story to produce the outcome.

**A Warning That Is Specific Enough to Be Checked**

The prediction is unusually concrete. Not a general misfortune but a bolt from the sky that turns the garden into a levelled, barren plain — a description that could be verified or falsified by looking at the ground.

The specificity serves the parable's argument. A vague warning about the fragility of wealth invites agreement without consequence; a named mechanism makes the claim testable, and the verses that follow report the test's outcome.

It also matches the sūrah's wider method. The story of the cave is built from checkable details — the sun's angle, the turning of bodies, the coins — and this parable uses the same technique on a smaller scale.

**"Better Than Your Garden"**

The phrase deserves a moment on its own. The poor man does not ask for the same garden, or for its restoration, or for an equal share. He speaks of something better than it.

The wording shows that he has not accepted the owner's scale of value even while conceding his figures. A better thing need not be a larger garden; the Qur'an's descriptions of what God gives use categories the owner cannot count.

The reader has already seen those categories. A few verses earlier the sūrah described bracelets of gold, green silk, and canopied couches as the reward of those who believe and do good. The companion's *better than your garden* is measured against that description, not against another plot of land.

The verse therefore leaves the comparison open in a productive way. The reader is not told what the better thing is, and the following verses will show that the owner's loss arrives first.

**The Same Reversal in Sūrat al-Qalam**

The parallel parable delivers the same ending by a different route. *“Indeed, We have tested those ˹Meccans˺ as We tested the owners of the garden—when they swore they would surely harvest ˹all˺ its fruit in the early morning”* (68:17).

In Sūrat al-Qalam the loss comes overnight to a group who had excluded the poor from their harvest; here it comes to one man who had excluded God from his accounting. Both gardens are destroyed, and in both cases the destruction follows a statement of certainty about the future.

The pairing establishes the rule the parable is teaching. Certainty about what one owns is treated in the Qur'an as the specific error that precedes loss.

Both parables also place the loss inside a single night, which compresses the interval between boast and outcome to the shortest the narrative allows.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:41

> Or its water may sink ˹into the earth˺, and then you will never be able to seek it out.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second of Two Named Disasters**

The warning began in the previous verse with a reckoning sent from the sky. This verse completes it with the opposite movement: the water going down instead of coming down.

Two mechanisms, opposite in direction, cover the estate completely. A garden can be destroyed from above by too much water arriving at once, or from below by the supply disappearing. The companion does not threaten in general terms; he names the two ways this particular property can fail.

The precision matters. A vague prediction about the fragility of wealth invites agreement and changes nothing. Two specific failure modes, both of them real, force the listener to consider that the warning has been thought about.

The pairing also covers time. A bolt from above can arrive in a single afternoon; a falling water table takes longer but is harder to reverse. Between them the two threats describe both the sudden and the gradual ending of an estate, and neither is avoidable by the owner's own effort.

**The One Thing He Could Not Duplicate**

The estate was described earlier as two gardens with a river running between them. Water was the single feature he could not manufacture. Palms can be planted, vines trained, trellises built, crops sown — but a river is not made.

That is why the warning targets the water. Everything else in the garden was arranged by human labour and could in principle be restored by human labour. The supply itself could not, and the verse identifies the dependence the owner had declined to acknowledge.

The parable has been building toward this. A man who says of his property that it will never perish has already excluded the possibility that its source might be withdrawn.
