The physical readings rest on reports without support in the text, and none of them explains why a ruler would be titled after his own anatomy.

**Alexander, Cyrus, or a Righteous Servant?**

The traditional commentaries most often identify him with Alexander of Macedon, on the basis of a report describing Dhū al-Qarnayn as a youth from the western lands who founded Alexandria. A minority of modern scholars have proposed Cyrus the Great.

The Qur'an itself declines to identify him. It gives no name, no lineage, no date, and no city, and it describes him only as a servant established in the land and given the means to all things.

The silence is deliberate and consistent with the account of the servant at the junction earlier in the sūrah. A figure defined by what God gave him rather than by who he was cannot be pinned to a dynasty, and the attempt to do so adds nothing the narrative requires.

**An Answer That Refuses the Terms of the Test**

The question was designed to determine whether the Prophet ﷺ possessed knowledge he could not have acquired. The answer gives an account, and the account is partial by announcement.

That is a significant way to respond to a test. A full biography would have satisfied the examiners' demand for information; a partial account shifts the ground from what the Prophet ﷺ knows to what has been revealed to him.

The wording makes the shift explicit. *I will relate to you something of his narrative* places the account in the category of recitation rather than knowledge, and the following verses supply exactly what was promised and nothing more.

The announcement also disarms the test in advance. A questioner who has been told he will receive something cannot complain that he did not receive everything, and the verse closes that avenue before the narrative begins.

**Why This Story Follows the Account of Moses**

The sūrah has just described a prophet travelling to the edge of the known world in search of knowledge he did not have. It now describes a ruler travelling to the edges of the earth with power he was given.

The contrast is the point of the sequence. Moses had knowledge and sought more; Dhū al-Qarnayn had power and used it. Neither account is a biography, and both are arranged around what God gave rather than what the man achieved.

The placement also answers the sūrah's opening claim. The earth's adornment was made as a test, and the third narrative shows a man given the largest share of that adornment who does not claim it as his own.

**The Same Restraint in the Sūrah's First Answer**

The account of the cave youths was given with the same limitation. The sūrah narrated what it narrated and then stated that only a few know their number, and instructed the Prophet ﷺ not to consult anyone about them (18:22).

Both answers refuse the completeness the questioners were testing for, and both state the refusal openly. The pattern identifies what the sūrah treats as revelation: a given account, bounded, and not expandable by enquiry.

Read together, the two responses answer the same test in the same way, and the third question in the set is answered elsewhere in the Qur'an in the same terms.

**The Third Question in the Series**

This verse opens the third subject of the chapter's long middle section. The cave youths were the first, the traveller of the two seas was the second, and now the man who crossed the earth is the third.

The order is not random. The first case deals with belief under persecution, the second with the limits of a prophet's knowledge, and the third with power exercised over the earth. Each is a different kind of test, and each is answered in a different register.

The introduction is also the shortest of the three. The account does not build up to the man; it names what he was given and moves immediately to the journeys, which is a treatment reserved for the subject the chapter wants to weigh rather than admire.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:84

> Surely We established him in the land, and gave him the means to all things

**Expanded Commentary**

**Established in the Land**

The verse opens with what God did rather than with what the man did. He was established in the earth — given a footing, made secure in it, able to move through it.

The commentators read the establishment as the land being made smooth and easy for him, so that he could travel it and bring it under control. The wording is therefore about capacity conferred rather than territory conquered.

The distinction is the verse's first claim. A ruler's reach is normally described in terms of what he took; here it is described in terms of what he was given, and the difference governs everything that follows in the account.

The second gift is expressed with a single word: *sabab* — a means, a way, a cause by which a thing is reached. He was given a *sabab* to everything.

The commentators read it broadly. Most take it to include knowledge of things, and al-Rāzī adds the power and the instruments by which what he wanted could be obtained. The word covers route, method, and capacity together.

That range explains the narrative that follows. A man given the means to all things can travel to the setting of the sun, reach a pass between two mountains, and build a barrier of iron and copper. Nothing in the account requires a resource he does not have.

**Everything Given, Nothing Claimed**

The grammar of the verse is the same grammar the sūrah used for the gardens. There, two gardens were given, palms were placed, a river was caused to flow; here the land is established and the means are given.

The parallel is exact and it is doing work. The garden owner received an estate and described it as his own. Dhū al-Qarnayn receives the earth and describes himself as a servant holding a trust.

The sūrah is comparing two recipients of the same kind of gift, and the comparison is decided by what each says about it. One said *I do not think I see anything better than this*; the other will say *this is a mercy from my Lord*.

The grammar is what makes the comparison fair. Both accounts use the same construction for the giving, so the difference cannot be attributed to the way the narrative frames them. It lies entirely in the words the two men are given.

That is the sūrah's method throughout. The description of a gift is kept neutral so that the recipient's own statement can carry the verdict.

**The Contrast With the Owner of the Gardens**

The two men are separated by forty verses and joined by a single question: what does a person say about what he has been given?

The garden owner's estate was small — two gardens, a river, some crops — and his claim was large. Dhū al-Qarnayn's establishment is described as extending across the earth, and his claim is that it is a mercy from his Lord and that its end is fixed.

The sūrah therefore measures the two by the same standard it applied at the start. The adornment was made as a test of which of its inhabitants is best in deeds, and the third narrative supplies the case that passes.

The case is also the largest available. No reader can argue that the test was easy for this man because his portion was small; the verse describes the portion as extending to everything.

**Sabab as the Word That Governs What Follows**

The term used here reappears as the verb of the journeys that follow. He followed a *sabab*, then another, then a third. The same word that describes his endowment describes his method of travel.

That repetition is structural. The means given are the means used, and the narrative shows a man operating entirely within what he was provided. Nothing in the three journeys is achieved by resources outside the endowment.

The word also carries the sense of a rope or a tether — something by which a person reaches what is otherwise out of reach. A ruler travelling to the edges of the earth is doing so along a line he did not make.

**A Servant Before a King**

The verse never calls him a king. It says he was established in the land and given means, both of which are things done to him rather than things he is.

That is consistent with the account's description of him elsewhere as one whom God loved and who loved God, and with the reports that describe him as a righteous servant granted sovereignty along with knowledge and wisdom. The title of the narrative is a description of what he held, not of what he was.

The narrative will confirm it in his own words. When the barrier is finished, the man who was given the earth attributes the work to his Lord and predicts its destruction.

**The Same Attribution in the Parable of the Gardens**

The sūrah's earlier parable used identical grammar for a much smaller gift: *“And We gave one of them two gardens of grapevines, surrounding them with palm trees, and placing crops between them”* (18:32).

Both accounts begin with what God gave. The difference is not in the size of the gift but in the description its recipient gives it, and the sūrah places the two cases far enough apart that the reader must supply the comparison.

Read together, they answer the question the seventh verse posed. The adornment is a test, and the two men are its two possible outcomes.

The verse describes two gifts separately. God established him in the land, and God gave him the means to all things. The first concerns position; the second concerns capacity.

The distinction matters because either could be held without the other. A man may hold a throne and lack the resources to act, or may possess resources without a settled place from which to use them. The verse gives both, and the account spends its remaining words on what the combination produced.

The wording also keeps both gifts in the passive. He was established and he was given, and no clause in the verse attributes either to his own doing, which is the same treatment the cave youths received when their hearts were fortified.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:85

> So he travelled a course

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Verse in the Sūrah**

The verse is three words in English and barely more in Arabic. It records a single movement and stops.

Its brevity is not a fragment. It is a complete verse with a subject, a verb, and an object, and it functions as the hinge between the endowment described in the previous verse and the destination described in the next.

The sūrah uses the same technique elsewhere. Short verses mark transitions and long verses carry content, and the alternation gives the narrative its rhythm. Here the shortest possible statement of travel precedes the most detailed description of a journey's end in the whole account.

The contrast in length is itself informative. The Qur'an spends three words on the crossing and a full verse on the arrival, which tells the reader where the account's weight lies.

The verb is built on the same root as the word used in the previous verse. He was given a *sabab* to all things, and here he follows a *sabab*.

The repetition ties the endowment to its use. The means given are not abstract capacity but an actual route, and the verse describes a man travelling along what he was provided rather than making his own way.

The word's range is worth holding. A *sabab* is a rope, a means, a cause, a way of reaching something. A traveller following a *sabab* is following a line laid down for him, and the narrative will use the same verb twice more.

**The Structure of the Three Journeys**

The account is built on three movements, each introduced by this verb. He followed a course, then followed a course, then followed a course — to the west, to the east, and to the pass between two mountains.

The repetition is structural rather than repetitive. Each journey begins with the same three words and ends with a different encounter, and the pattern lets the reader track the geography without the narrative having to explain it.

The three destinations also cover the whole earth. The setting of the sun, the rising of the sun, and a pass between two mountains leave no direction unvisited, which is what the title promised and what the endowment made possible.

The third destination is the odd one. It is not an extremity but an obstacle, and the account gives it more space than the two extremities together, which tells the reader where the narrative's interest lies.

**Why the Verse Is So Short**

The travel is not the subject of the account. What matters is what he found and what he did when he arrived, and the verse refuses to spend words on the movement between.

That economy is characteristic of Qur'anic narrative. Journeys that would fill chapters in other literatures are compressed into a clause, and the space saved is given to speech — the exchanges with the people he meets take up the verses that follow.

The verse therefore performs a function rather than conveying information. It moves the narrative from the general endowment to the particular case, and it does so without commentary.

**The Direction of the First Journey**

The verse that follows identifies the destination as the setting place of the sun. The first journey is therefore westward, toward the edge of the inhabited world as that account describes it.

The choice of the west first is not arbitrary. The narrative moves from the place where the sun goes down to the place where it comes up, which is the order in which a day ends and begins, and the sequence covers the full circuit.

The verse itself gives no direction, and the omission is typical. The travel is stated, and the destination is supplied when it is reached.

The effect is to keep the reader's attention on arrivals rather than routes. Nothing in the account depends on where the road ran, and the verse declines to invent detail that would invite speculation about geography the narrative does not care about.

The verse's real content is what it does not say. It does not describe an army, a fleet, supplies, or planning. A man follows a means, and the next verse reports where he arrived.

The absence of detail is consistent with the previous verse. A person given the means to all things does not need to organise a journey; the means are the organisation.

That is the account's quiet claim about power. It is not accumulated by the one who holds it, and the narrative records its use in the same terms in which it recorded its giving.

**The Same Verb in the Journeys That Follow**

The account repeats the construction twice more, marking the second and third journeys in identical terms before describing what was found at the end of each.

The repetition establishes the shape of the whole narrative: a means followed, a place reached, a people met, a decision made. Three times, with the third producing the barrier that occupies the rest of the account.

The verse is therefore the first instance of a pattern the reader learns to recognise, and its brevity is what makes the pattern visible rather than stated.

The verse contains three words of content: he followed a means. Every other verse in this section describes a place, a people, or an encounter; this one describes only movement.

The brevity is doing work. By reducing travel to a formula, the account declines to make the journeys interesting in themselves and reserves its attention for what happens at the ends of them.

The formula is also repeated identically three times, which turns it into a refrain. A refrain teaches the reader to expect what follows it, and what follows is always an encounter rather than an adventure.

**What the Verse Leaves Out**

The verse names no place, no distance, no hardship, and no companion. A man travels from one extremity of the earth to another and the account records three words about it.

The omission is a decision about what the chapter considers worth knowing. The journeys matter only as the means by which the traveller reaches the people he meets, and the verse refuses to make them interesting on their own account.

The same restraint governs the whole account. The account of Dhū al-Qarnayn contains no description of his court, his army, or his reign, because none of it bears on the question the chapter is answering. What is recorded is what he was given, where he went, and what he did when he arrived.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:86

> until he reached the setting ˹point˺ of the sun, which appeared to him to be setting in a spring of murky water, where he found some people. We said, “O Ⱬul-Qarnain! Either punish them or treat them kindly.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Westernmost Point of the Journey**

The first journey ends at the setting place of the sun — the furthest west the traveller reached, described from the perspective of someone standing at the edge of the inhabited land.

The wording is that of an observer, not of an astronomer. The sun has a *setting place* in the same way it has a horizon: a point at which, from where the observer stands, it goes out of sight.

The commentators read the verse this way consistently. What is described is how the setting sun appeared to the human eye at that location, and the description is the traveller's rather than a claim about the sun's motion.

The spring is described as dark or turbid, and the standard explanation is geographical. At the westernmost edge of the land, the ocean stretched to the horizon; as the sun descended it appeared to enter the water, and the distant sea looked black.

That reading is supported by the wording itself. The verse says the sun appeared to him to be setting there, which locates the description in the traveller's sight rather than in the nature of the sun.

The detail is not decorative. A dark body of water at the edge of the world is what a traveller would actually report, and the account keeps the report in those terms rather than correcting it into a cosmology.

**A People Found There**

The verse records the second thing he found: a people. The reports about them are extravagant — a city of twelve thousand gates is mentioned in the commentaries — but the verse itself gives no description beyond their presence.

The context supplies what matters. The choice offered in the second half of the verse implies that the people were in a state of disbelief or idolatry, since the alternative to punishment is a call to something better.

The narrative's restraint is consistent. It names the people, states that they were found, and moves to the decision about them. Nothing is said about their numbers, their city, or their customs.

The economy is deliberate. The account is not ethnography; it is a record of how delegated authority behaves on encountering people who have not been addressed before. Everything the verse omits is omitted because it does not bear on that question.

**A Choice Offered From Outside**

The verse's second half is a direct address: O Dhū al-Qarnayn, either punish them or treat them kindly. The options are given to him rather than devised by him.

The first option is severe — the commentators read it as killing those who persisted in idolatry. The second is literally to take a way of kindness toward them, which the exegetes explain as calling them to submission to the one God and seeking their guidance, or simply treating them generously.

The pairing is significant. A ruler at the edge of the world, encountering an idolatrous people, is given two legitimate courses and asked to choose. The verse does not predetermine the choice.

The delegation is also complete. Nothing in the wording limits the punishment or defines the kindness; both are left to his judgment, which is the largest discretion the narrative grants him anywhere.

God speaks to him in this verse, and the fact has been used as an argument about his standing. If he received direct instruction, the commentators reason, he received a form of revelation, which supports the position that he was a prophet.

Others hold that the instruction came through inspiration rather than revelation, which would leave his status as a righteous ruler rather than a prophet.

The argument cannot be settled from the text. The Qur'an describes God speaking to him and does not classify the communication, and both readings remain open. What the verse establishes beyond dispute is that his authority in this matter was delegated rather than assumed.

**What the Choice Reveals About His Rule**

The two options define the character of the authority he holds. Punishment is available, and so is kindness, and the decision between them is his.

That is a significant description of power. A ruler given the earth is not thereby given unlimited discretion; he is given a choice between two courses, both of which are legitimate, and the following verses record which he chose and how he explained it.

The account is therefore not a celebration of conquest. It is a case study in how delegated authority is exercised, and the choice made is the subject of the next two verses.

The sūrah has described authority in these terms before. The servant at the junction acted on command and said so explicitly: I did not do it on my own (18:82).

Dhū al-Qarnayn occupies the same position at a larger scale. He holds the earth and its means, and the first decision the narrative records him making is one whose options were given to him from outside.

The parallel is the sūrah's answer to the garden owner. Two men were given what they held; one described it as his own, and the other waited to be told what to do with it.

**A Finding, Not an Observation**

The verse says he found the sun setting in a murky spring. The verb is one of discovery, and the subject of the finding is the traveller rather than the sun.

That grammatical arrangement is the reason the verse cannot be read as a statement of astronomy. Nothing is asserted about the sun's motion; something is reported about what a man saw from a particular place at a particular hour.

The account is consistent on this point throughout. The eastern people are described by what he found, not by what they are, and the western people are addressed by what he decided. In every case the narrative records the encounter rather than the geography.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:87

> He responded, “Whoever does wrong will be punished by us, then will be returned to their Lord, Who will punish them with a horrible torment

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Branch of the Answer**

Dhū al-Qarnayn responds to the choice with a statement of policy rather than a decision about a particular group. Whoever does wrong will be punished by us.

The form is general and conditional. No one is named, no sentence is pronounced in advance, and the outcome is made to depend on what the people do after the call reaches them.

That is the shape of a just announcement. The warning precedes the judgment, and the judgment is conditioned on conduct that has not yet occurred.

The first branch is therefore not a threat issued by the ruler but a consequence he is authorised to impose, and the verse is careful to keep the two distinct. What he does is bounded; what follows it is not his to decide.

**"The One Who Does Wrong"**

The commentators identify the wrong referred to here as association with God — persisting in idolatry after being called to the worship of one God.

The identification follows from the sequence. The choice offered in the previous verse concerned a people in a state of disbelief, and the answer addresses what happens to those who remain in it. The wrong is therefore specified by the context rather than defined in the verse.

The wording is nevertheless general, and the generality is deliberate. A policy stated as applying to whoever does wrong is a standard rather than a threat aimed at particular individuals, and it leaves the determination of guilt to the conduct that follows the call.

**Two Punishments in One Verse**

The verse describes a punishment in this world and a punishment in the next. The first is inflicted by the ruler; the second by God.

The commentators read the first as execution for persisting in disbelief and the second as the torment of the Hereafter. The two are distinguished by their agents and by their order, and the verse is careful to keep them separate.

The separation matters. A ruler who conflated the two would be claiming an authority he does not hold; the verse gives him the first and assigns the second elsewhere.

The pairing also explains why the verse is short. A single sentence carries both the ruler's jurisdiction and its outer limit, and neither can be removed without losing half of the claim.

**"Then He Will Be Returned to His Lord"**

The middle clause is the verse's hinge. After the earthly punishment, the person is returned to his Lord, and the returning is described as a transfer rather than an end.

The wording places a limit on the ruler's jurisdiction. What he does is preliminary; what follows belongs to another, and the verse says so before describing it.

The placement of the clause is what makes it a limit rather than an addition. It comes between the two punishments rather than after them, which means the ruler's penalty is described as interrupted by the return to God rather than completed by it.

That structure is unusual in a statement of royal policy. A ruler announcing his penalties would ordinarily stop at the penalty. This one continues past his own authority and describes what happens after it.

**A Ruler Who Does Not Claim the Final Word**

The verse is the first recorded speech of Dhū al-Qarnayn, and its content is a disclaimer of ultimacy. He punishes, and then God punishes, and the second punishment is described as horrible in a way the first is not.

The comparison is implicit and unflattering to the ruler's own power. What he can do is limited and temporary; what follows is neither.

That is the account's portrait of legitimate authority. A man given the means to all things describes his own penalties as the lesser of two, and assigns the greater to someone else.

The account therefore gives its most powerful figure a deliberately restricted role. He commands, judges, and builds, and in every case the verse records the point at which his authority ends.

**The Contrast With the Garden Owner**

The sūrah has already recorded a man describing his own position in the largest available terms: I do not think I see anything better than this, and I do not think the Hour will ever come (18:35–36).

Dhū al-Qarnayn's first speech does the opposite. He asserts a limited authority and affirms the return to God and the punishment that follows it. The two statements are the sūrah's paired measures of what a person says when he holds power.

The difference is not in the size of what each holds. It is in what each says about its limits.

The contrast is drawn without either man being named in the other's story. The account relies on the reader holding both cases at once, which is how the chapter makes most of its arguments.

**The Sequence the Verse Establishes**

The order of the verse is the order of the Qur'an's own teaching. A warning is given, a response is made, a worldly consequence follows, and a final reckoning comes after.

Nothing in the sequence is collapsed. The earthly penalty does not exhaust the matter, and the final reckoning does not replace it. Both are stated, in order, by a ruler describing what he has been authorised to do.

The verse therefore functions as a model of delegated judgment, and the following verse supplies the other branch of the same policy.

The two branches together describe a complete system: an invitation, a consequence for refusal, a reward for acceptance, and a promise about the weight of the demands that follow. Every element is announced before it is applied, which is what distinguishes it from the exercise of raw power.

The account's interest in the speech rather than in the conquest is now clear. A ruler could have been described by the extent of his rule; this one is described by the terms he states.

**Two Judgments Offered Together**

The verse gives the ruler two options in a single clause: punish, or treat with kindness. Neither is prescribed, and no guidance is offered about which to choose.

The pairing is unusual. Most accounts of authority describe what a ruler did; this one describes what he was permitted to do, and leaves the choice inside the verse.

The permission is also bounded by the second half, which says those who wrong themselves will be returned to their Lord. The ruler's jurisdiction covers this life, and the verse states the limit at the same moment it states the options.

The absence of guidance is itself informative. A reader expecting a rule is given a range, and the verse assumes the reader can hold that range without needing it closed.

**Authority Stated as a Choice**

The verse is unusual in that it records a permission rather than an action. The ruler is told what he may do, and the account does not say which of the two he chose in any given case.

That openness is the verse's subject. Power over a people is presented as a range of lawful options rather than as a single prescribed course, and the boundary is drawn not by the options but by what lies beyond them.

The boundary is stated in the same breath: those who wrong themselves will be returned to their Lord. The ruler's authority is real, it is wide, and it stops where the next life begins. The verse gives all three facts in one sentence.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:88

> As for those who believe and do good, they will have the finest reward, and we will assign them easy commands.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Branch of the Policy**

The verse completes the answer begun in the previous one. The wrongdoer is punished; the one who believes and does good receives the finest reward and an easy command.

The pairing is the standard Qur'anic formula. Belief and righteous action are joined throughout the Book as the condition of reward, and the verse uses the same construction a ruler would use in stating his policy to a new people.

The form is conditional in both branches. Neither outcome is assigned in advance; both depend on the response the people make to the call.

The construction also puts the two groups on the same footing. Neither is favoured before the invitation reaches them, and the difference between them arises entirely afterwards.

The phrase translated as the finest reward is *al-ḥusnā* — the most beautiful, the best. The commentators read it in two ways.

The first takes the beauty as a quality of the reward itself: the reward they receive is the best available. The second reads it as a description of the deed rewarded — the finest deed, which some identify with the testimony of God's oneness and others with believing and doing good together.

The first reading is the more natural fit for the grammar, since the word qualifies what they will have rather than what they did. The second is not excluded, and both agree on the substance: what these people receive is of the highest order.

**"Easy Commands"**

The second promise concerns what the ruler will require of them. The commentators explain it concretely: he will impose the obligations of charity and land tax at a level and in a manner they can bear.

Others read it more generally as a promise of kind treatment. Both readings describe the same policy — that the demands placed on the believing group will be light rather than burdensome.

The specification is significant. A ruler stating his terms to a newly encountered people names the fiscal obligations first, and promises that they will be easy. The verse treats the weight of a demand as a matter of justice rather than of convenience.

The promise is also a constraint on himself. A ruler who announces that his demands will be light has bound himself in advance, and the announcement is made to the people who will be subject to it rather than kept as a private intention.

**The Contrast Between the Two Branches**

The two verses describe severity and ease, and the difference between them is entirely a function of the people's response. Nothing about the ruler changes between them.

That is the point of the pairing. Authority is exercised differently toward those who accept the call and those who refuse it, and the difference is disclosed in advance so that neither outcome can be described as arbitrary.

The verse also answers a question the account invites. A man given the means to all things could impose anything; the narrative shows him announcing that his demands on the believing group will be the lightest available.

Read together, the two verses describe a complete policy: an invitation, a warning, a reward, and a promise of light obligations. Every element is stated before it is applied.

The sequence is the Qur'an's own standard for judgment. No people are punished before being addressed, and no reward is given without a condition attached. The ruler at the edge of the world is described as observing both.

That is the verse's real argument. The furthest reach of the earth is governed by the same procedure as the nearest, and the procedure is disclosed rather than improvised.

That is why the account includes the speech at all. The narrative is not interested in the extent of his travels; it is interested in what he said when he arrived.

**The Same Formula Across the Qur'an**

The pairing of belief and righteous deeds as the condition of reward appears throughout the Book, and the sūrah has already used it. Those who believe and do good will have the Gardens of Paradise as an accommodation is stated later in this same chapter, and the same formula occurs at 2:25, 3:57, 4:57, and elsewhere.

The recurrence identifies it as a fixed condition rather than a local arrangement. A ruler at the western edge of the earth states the same terms that the Qur'an states everywhere, which is what makes his policy recognisable as just.

The verse therefore does more than record a decision. It places a distant ruler inside the same order the sūrah has been describing from its first verse.

**The Reward Described Elsewhere in the Sūrah**

The sūrah has already described what the finest reward consists of: bracelets of gold, green garments of fine silk, and reclining on canopied couches (18:31).

The ruler's promise uses the same superlative without describing its content, and the reader supplies the description from earlier in the chapter. The account does not need to repeat it.

That economy is typical of the sūrah. Its terms are established once and then reused, and a reader who has followed the chapter recognises the phrase without further explanation.

The verse describes the reward for faith and good action as the easier path. Not a greater reward, not a higher station — an easier way.

The choice of reward is significant in a chapter about wealth. The garden owner measured his standing by what he possessed; this verse measures the believer's by what he no longer has to struggle with.

The wording also echoes the language of burden. The commands laid on these people are described as light, and the ease is presented as a consequence of the belief rather than as a separate gift, which makes the two halves of the verse one claim.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:89

> Then he travelled a ˹different˺ course

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second of Three Identical Statements**

The verse repeats the formula of verse 85 almost exactly. He followed a means. Three words, no destination, no description of the journey.

The repetition is not careless. The account uses the same sentence to open each of the three journeys, and the sameness is what allows the destinations to stand out.

A reader encountering the phrase for the second time recognises it as a marker. The narrative is signalling a transition rather than narrating travel, and the recognition makes the transition instantaneous.

The recognition is part of the design. A formula used twice becomes a signal, and the reader stops asking where the traveller is going and waits for the arrival instead.

**Turning From West to East**

The first journey ended at the setting place of the sun. This one, the following verse explains, ends at the rising place, and the circuit of the earth is completed in the opposite direction.

The verse itself says nothing about direction, and the omission is consistent with its function. It records movement; the destination is supplied when it is reached.

The order is nevertheless significant. The account moves from the westernmost point to the easternmost, which covers the full extent of the inhabited world as the narrative describes it, and leaves nothing between them untraversed.

The circuit also matches the title. A man called the one of the two horns travels to the two horns of the earth, and the second journey completes what the name promised.

**Why the Verse Repeats**

A narrative could have compressed the three journeys into one sentence. The Qur'an separates them, and the separation gives each destination its own approach.

The effect is measured. Each journey is introduced identically and concluded differently, which means the reader's attention is directed entirely to what is found rather than to how the traveller got there.

The technique also establishes a rhythm that the third journey will break. Two journeys follow the same pattern; the third arrives at a place where something must be built, and the narrative expands to accommodate it.

The repetition therefore does the work a description would otherwise do. By refusing to vary the formula, the account tells the reader that nothing in the journey deserves separate attention.

**The Means Followed Again**

The word is the same one used for what he was given: a *sabab*, a means, a way of reaching something. He was given means to all things, and here he follows a means again.

The repetition ties the endowment to its use across the whole account. Nothing in the three journeys is accomplished by resources outside what was provided, and the narrative keeps saying so in the same word.

The word's ordinary sense is a rope or a tether — something by which a person reaches what is otherwise out of reach. A ruler crossing the earth is described as travelling along a line he did not make.

The phrase reappears unchanged for the third time, and its stability is what marks the verse as a hinge rather than as another stage of travel.

**The Narrative's Rhythm**

The sūrah alternates between long verses carrying speech and short verses carrying movement. This is one of the short ones, and its brevity is functional.

The pattern is visible across the account: a means followed, a place reached, a people met, a decision announced. The first two elements are stated in a clause each, and the space is given to the exchanges that follow.

That distribution tells the reader what the account values. Travel is the least interesting part of the story, and the Qur'an allocates words accordingly, giving three words to each crossing and dozens to each exchange.

The allocation is itself an argument. A narrative about a ruler could have been a narrative about distance; this one is a narrative about encounters, and the short verses are how the difference is enforced.

**A Journey Between Two Known Points**

The verse occupies the space between a departure and an arrival, and it declines to fill that space with anything. No distances, no durations, no difficulties.

The refusal is consistent with the account's treatment of the first journey, which was also stated in three words. The narrative is not interested in the road; it is interested in what the road leads to.

The effect on the reader is to make each arrival feel sudden. Two journeys are announced identically, and each ends somewhere unexpected, which is the shape the account wants.

The sameness of the openings also prevents the journeys from being ranked. Neither west nor east is privileged by the narrative, and the third destination is given more space only because more happens there.

**One Verb Opening the Third Journey**

The construction appears once more before the account reaches the pass between the two mountains, where the barrier is built.

The three identical openings frame the three destinations, and the third is the one that occupies the rest of the sūrah. The verse is therefore the middle term of a structure whose final element is the longest.

The progression is deliberate. Two short journeys establish the pattern, and the third breaks it, which is how the narrative signals that the barrier matters more than the travel that preceded it.

The unchanged wording is the point. The third journey is not presented as harder, longer, or more remarkable than the first two, and the identical phrasing enforces that reading.

**The Circuit Closed**

The verse records the second turn of the journey. Having reached the west, the traveller is described turning east, and the account notes the movement without describing the road.

The turn completes a figure the chapter has been drawing. A man given the earth travels to one extremity, then to the other, and the two endpoints are the only places the narrative describes in detail.

The symmetry is the point. The account is not a travelogue; it is a survey, and a survey is complete when both ends have been reached, which is what this verse declares in the fewest words available.

The closing of the circuit is what allows the account to turn from survey to encounter. Once both extremities have been reached, the chapter can spend its remaining words on the barrier.

**The Turn Toward the East**

The verse records a change of direction and nothing else. Having reached the west, the traveller follows a means eastward, and the account offers no description of the road.

The change of direction completes the survey the chapter has been describing. A man established in the land and given the means to all things is shown moving between the two extremities, and the verse marks the moment the circuit closes.

The brevity also preserves the pattern established earlier. Each journey is given the same three words, and no journey receives more description than another, which keeps the reader's attention on the arrivals rather than on the travel.

The eastward turn is recorded in the same terms as the westward one, and the symmetry is what makes the third arrival feel like a destination rather than another stop.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:90

> until he reached the rising ˹point˺ of the sun. He found it rising on a people for whom We had provided no shelter from it

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Easternmost Point**

The second journey ends where the first began in reverse: the rising place of the sun, the furthest east the traveller reached.

The wording matches the earlier description exactly in form. There the sun appeared to set in a dark spring; here it appears to rise over a people. Both descriptions are given from the standpoint of an observer at the edge of the inhabited land.

The symmetry completes the circuit. The narrative has now covered the full span of the earth as it describes it, from the place the sun leaves to the place it returns.

The extremity is named and nothing further is said about it, which is consistent with the treatment of the western point several verses earlier.

What he found is unusual and specific: a people for whom no shelter from the sun had been provided.

The commentators describe the land as having no mountains and no trees to give natural shade, and no buildings or other structures to give artificial shelter. The people are exposed, and the exposure is total.

Both sources of shade are absent at once, which is what makes the description unusual. Most inhabited land has one or the other; this has neither, and the verse treats the combination as the people's defining condition.

The verse's wording is precise about the source of the condition. It says *We had not made* shelter for them, which attributes their situation to a provision withheld rather than to a failure of their own.

**Why They Had No Shelter**

The commentators offer two explanations. Some hold that the land could not sustain buildings; others that the people had no knowledge of how to build them.

The two readings differ in where they locate the limitation — in the environment or in the people — and the verse does not decide between them. It states only that no shelter existed.

The first reading has some support in the description of the land, which is presented as lacking the features that would make construction possible. The second rests on reports about the people rather than on the text.

The verse declines to explain the absence. It records the condition and leaves its cause unexamined, which is the same restraint the account shows toward the western spring.

**Living by the Movement of the Sun**

The reports describe how the people managed. When the sun rose they took refuge in water or burrowed underground, and when it passed its highest point and began to descend they emerged.

The account places this detail outside the verse, and the verse itself says nothing about how they coped. What it records is the condition, not the adaptation.

That restraint is typical. The narrative identifies a people by a single distinguishing feature and leaves the rest to the reader, which keeps the account moving toward the encounter rather than lingering on the description.

The single feature is also the one that matters for what follows. A people with no shelter are a people who cannot protect themselves, and the next encounter in the account is with people in the same condition asking for a wall.

**The Same Mandate Given Again**

Some commentators hold that Dhū al-Qarnayn was given the same instruction regarding this people as he received at the western point — to punish or to treat kindly.

The verse does not record any such address, and the narrative moves on to the third journey without describing an exchange. The reading therefore rests on the parallel structure rather than on the text.

What the verse does establish is that the journey reached a people, which is the same pattern as the first journey. Whether the same choice was offered is left unstated.

The repetition of the mandate is what keeps the account coherent. Each arrival is governed by the same instruction, and the verse records that instruction without restating its terms.

Between them the two journeys cover the earth from end to end. The narrative has described the westernmost and the easternmost points, and the third journey that follows goes somewhere else entirely — to a pass between two mountains.

The structure is therefore not a simple traverse. Two extremities are covered, and then the account turns inward to a place defined not by direction but by an obstacle.

The verse marks the end of the geographic survey and the beginning of the account's real subject, which is what a ruler does when he finds people who cannot protect themselves.

With both ends of the earth described, the account has established the scope of the endowment, and the remaining verses can be spent on a single encounter.

**A People Defined by Exposure**

The verse identifies this people by what they lack rather than by what they are. No city, no number, no custom — only the absence of shelter from the sun.

That identification prepares the third encounter. A people described as exposed are a people who need protection, and the account's final act is the construction of a barrier for people in exactly that condition.

The parallel is not stated in the verse, but the narrative places the two encounters close enough that the reader supplies it.

The definition is therefore negative, and the negativity is the verse's contribution. Everything the account says about these people follows from the single thing they do not have.

The wording *We had provided no shelter* uses the same construction the Qur'an uses for natural provision generally. Sūrat al-Naḥl describes shade and shelter among the things God made: *“And Allah has made for you shade from what He has created, and He has made for you shelters in the mountains”* (16:81).

The parallel identifies what this people lack as a normal part of provision. Shelter is not an achievement but a gift, and its absence is described as a withholding.

Read against that verse, the people at the rising place are defined by a deprivation the rest of the world does not notice, and the account uses the deprivation to set up what follows.

**The Only People Described by What They Lack**

Every other people in this section is identified by a place or an action. This one is identified by an absence: they had nothing to shelter them from the sun.

The description is deliberately minimal, and its minimalism is what the verse is about. A people without shade have no protection of their own, and the account gives them that one characteristic because it explains everything that follows.

The same idea appears elsewhere in the Qur'an, where God is described as making shade and shelters in the mountains (16:81). The verse here records the opposite condition, and the contrast is what makes the encounter worth telling.

The verse identifies a people by a single deprivation, and the deprivation is shelter from the sun. Not poverty, not weakness, not distance from God — exposure.

The choice is telling. Shade is the most basic form of protection a landscape can offer, and a people without it are described as having nothing of their own to fall back on.

The same measure appears elsewhere in the Qur'an, where God is described as providing shade and shelters in the mountains (16:81). The verse here records the absence of that provision, and the absence is what makes the people worth describing in a chapter concerned with what is given and what is taken away.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:91

> So it was. And We truly had full knowledge of him

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Journey Closed in Four Words**

The verse ends the second journey in the briefest terms available. So it was. Nothing is added about the eastern people, no exchange is recorded, and no decision is reported.

The compression is striking after the detailed account of the western encounter. There, a choice was offered and a policy was stated across three verses; here the arrival is noted and the narrative moves on.

The asymmetry tells the reader something about the account's priorities. The western encounter involved a decision about people; the eastern one, as the Qur'an tells it, involved an observation.

The difference is not a gap in the record so much as a statement about what the narrative considers worth telling. Two peoples are described; one is addressed. The verse closes the second case in four words because nothing in it required a reply, and the account moves on to the encounter that does.

**"We Had Full Knowledge of What Was Before Him"**

The second clause states that God encompassed in knowledge what was before him. The phrase has been read in two directions.

The first takes what was before him as the people he encountered — the strange nation at the rising place, about whom the Qur'an has said very little. On this reading the verse supplies what the narrative withheld: God knew them completely.

The second takes it as what was with him — his armies, his equipment, and the means he carried. The verse then describes God's knowledge of the ruler's own resources.

Both readings are supported by the wording, and the verse does not choose. The common ground is that the knowledge described is God's, not the traveller's.

Whichever reading is taken, the clause places the ruler inside a knowledge he does not possess. He was given the means to all things, and the verse immediately states that what lay before him was encompassed by another's awareness.

The sequence is deliberate. An endowment is described, the endowment is used across two journeys, and then the account notes that the whole of it sits inside a knowledge greater than the ruler's.

That is the same structure the sūrah applied to the servant at the junction. He held knowledge from God's presence, and the account closed by comparing his knowledge and Moses' to what a sparrow takes from the sea.

**Why the Verse Appears Here**

The clause is placed between the second journey and the third, at the point where the narrative has covered the earth from end to end.

The placement gives the survey its frame. Two extremities have been reached and described, and the verse states that everything in them was already known to someone other than the traveller.

The effect is to prevent the journeys from reading as achievements. A man who reaches the edges of the earth has not discovered anything that was unknown, and the verse says so before the third journey begins.

The timing is exact. The claim about knowledge is inserted at the moment the survey is complete, which is when a reader would be most inclined to admire the traveller. The verse removes the ground for that admiration before the account turns to the barrier.

The verse answers a claim the sūrah has already examined. The garden owner described his property as permanent and himself as its source; this verse describes a ruler at the earth's extremities whose surroundings are encompassed by a knowledge that is not his.

The two men are the sūrah's paired cases. One held a small estate and claimed it as his own; the other holds the earth and is described as standing inside someone else's knowledge.

The comparison is not stated, and it does not need to be. The verse makes the point by placing the knowledge where it belongs.

The placement is also the account's answer to the question the Makkans were asking. A test designed to measure the Prophet's knowledge is answered with a story whose largest figure is defined by what he does not know, and by the One who does.

**The Same Framing in the Sūrah's Opening**

The sūrah began by describing a Book with no crookedness in it, made straight so that it might warn of a severe punishment from God (18:1–2). The knowledge claimed there is the same knowledge asserted here.

A ruler given the means to all things is encompassed by it, and the account says so in four words. The narrative's largest figure is placed inside the same frame as its smallest.

That is the verse's function. It closes a survey of the earth by restating who knows it.

The verse closes its second case with almost nothing. A people is mentioned, a statement is made about what they had, and the account moves on.

The economy is not carelessness. The western encounter required a decision and received several verses; the eastern one required nothing and received a clause. The narrative allocates its words according to what each case demands.

That allocation is also a judgment. The chapter's interest has never been in places but in what people do with what they are given, and a people with no shelter and no request produces no case to decide.

**A Case Closed Without a Decision**

The verse ends its second case in a clause. A people is mentioned, a fact about them is stated, and the account turns to the third journey.

No judgment is passed, no instruction is given, and no exchange is recorded. The narrative simply notes what it found and leaves it there, which is a treatment the chapter gives to nothing else in this section.

That treatment is itself informative. The account describes encounters according to what they require, and an encounter that requires nothing is closed quickly. The reader is told that the survey was thorough without being shown every stop along it.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:92

> Then he travelled a ˹third˺ course

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Formula Used a Third Time**

The verse repeats the two preceding transitions word for word. He followed a means. Three words, no direction, no destination.

By the third use the formula has become a signal rather than a statement. The reader recognises it as the marker of a new journey and waits for the arrival, which the narrative supplies in the following verse.

The repetition is therefore functional. It saves the account from having to describe travel three times, and it trains the reader to expect what comes after rather than what comes during.

The training is efficient. By the third use, three words are enough to close one episode and open another, and the narrative can spend its space on the encounter that occupies the rest of the chapter.

The formula is also honest about what it does not know. The verse records movement without inventing detail about it, which is the same restraint the account shows toward the places it does describe.

**The Turn Away From the Extremities**

The first two journeys ended at the setting and the rising of the sun — the two furthest points in opposite directions. The third ends somewhere else entirely: a pass between two mountains.

The shift is significant. A narrative that had covered the earth from end to end now turns to a location defined not by direction but by an obstacle. The geography stops being a survey and becomes a problem.

The verse gives no hint of the change. It uses the same three words as before, and the difference appears only in what is found at the end.

Turning away from the edges is what allows the narrative to move inward, and the inward movement is where the chapter's third subject actually takes place.

**Why Three Journeys**

Two journeys would have covered the earth. The third is not needed for the circuit, and its inclusion tells the reader that the circuit was never the point.

The first two establish the ruler's reach; the third establishes what he does with it. Everything the narrative is interested in — the request, the refusal of payment, the construction, the prophecy of its end — happens at the third destination.

The structure is therefore weighted. Two short journeys build expectation, and the third receives more verses than both together.

The weighting is also a judgment about what matters. Extremities are interesting as geography; a mountain gap with frightened people on the near side is interesting as a problem, and the account gives its words to the problem.

The third journey is also the only one that produces something permanent. The first two end in observation and the third ends in a construction that the sūrah describes in engineering detail, which is where the narrative's attention has been heading all along.

**The Means Followed Once More**

The word is the same one used for the endowment: a *sabab*, a means by which something is reached. He was given means to all things, and each journey is described as following one.

The consistency is the account's quiet claim. Nothing in the three journeys is accomplished by improvisation or by resources outside what was provided, and the narrative keeps saying so in the same term.

The word's ordinary sense is a rope or tether. A ruler crossing the earth is described each time as travelling along a line laid down for him, which is the same claim the endowment made in different words.

The consistency across all three journeys is what makes the claim credible. A single instance could be coincidence; three identical descriptions of travel along a given means are a pattern the narrative is asserting rather than observing.

**What the Repetition Signals**

Three identical openings before three different arrivals produce a specific effect: the journeys feel interchangeable and the destinations do not.

That is the distribution the account wants. The travel is routine; what is found is not. By the third repetition the reader has stopped attending to the movement entirely, which is exactly the state the narrative needs before introducing the people who will ask for a barrier.

The verse is therefore doing preparation rather than narration. It clears the ground so that the encounter can occupy it.

The signal is therefore structural rather than descriptive. The formula marks the boundary between episodes, and the reader learns to treat it as a divider rather than as information.

The construction appears at verse 85, at verse 89, and here, and each instance is followed by a verse describing what was found.

The pattern is consistent enough that the reader can predict the shape of what follows. The third instance is followed by the longest arrival in the account, which is how the narrative marks the barrier as the subject of the story.

The verse's brevity is what makes that marking work. Had it been longer, the arrival would not stand out against it.

The account's economy is therefore a rhetorical instrument. Three words of travel, and then a people who can barely speak asking a ruler to build them a barrier — the disproportion is the point.

**The Third Turn**

The verse opens the third movement with the same three words used twice before. He followed a means, and the account does not vary the formula.

The unvaried repetition marks the arrival as the significant element. By the third use the reader has learned to treat the phrase as a signal, and everything after it in this section carries the chapter's weight.

The verse is therefore the shortest in the account and the one that precedes its longest passage. That disproportion is deliberate, and it directs attention to the encounter rather than to the road.

The third turn is also the last. After it the account stops describing movement altogether and devotes its remaining verses to one place and one conversation.

The verse divides its attention unevenly. The travel receives three words; the arrival receives the rest of the section.

The division reflects the chapter's priorities. What a man does when he reaches a place is worth recording; how he got there is not, and the verse enforces that distinction by refusing to describe the road even once.

The pattern holds across all three journeys. Each is introduced with the identical formula and each is followed by an encounter described at length, which is how the account keeps a long middle section moving without losing its subject.

The skipping is consistent. No road in this section is described, and the uniformity of the omission is what gives the arrivals their weight.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:93

> until he reached ˹a pass˺ between two mountains. He found in front of them a people who could hardly understand ˹his˺ language

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Place Between Two Barriers**

The third journey ends at a location described by its position: between two mountains, in the opening between them.

The commentators most often identify the two mountains with the ranges of Armenia and Azerbaijan, which formed a barrier to the lands lying further north. The identification is a report rather than a statement of the text, and the Qur'an gives no names.

What the verse establishes is the shape of the place. A gap between two ranges is a route, and a route is what makes the people living there vulnerable. The geography is the reason the encounter that follows is necessary.

The same geography determines the solution. A gap can be filled; a range cannot be moved. The construction described in the following verses uses the mountains themselves as the two sides of the work, which is why the location had to be described before the request could make sense.

**A People Who Could Hardly Understand Speech**

The second clause describes the people by a deficiency: they could barely comprehend speech. The commentators read it in two ways.

The first takes it as a linguistic barrier — they spoke a language no one else understood and understood no other. The second takes it as a difficulty of comprehension generally, so that they could follow others only with effort or through gestures.

The first reading fits the wording more closely, since the verse describes comprehension of speech rather than intelligence. The second is broader and rests on the same reports.

The difficulty is therefore a fact about the encounter rather than about the people. It shapes how the request is made and how briefly it is recorded.

Despite the description, the people speak to Dhū al-Qarnayn in the following verse and he answers them. The commentators offer two explanations: God gave him the ability to understand their language, as He gave Solomon the language of the birds (27:16), or an interpreter was present.

The verse does not resolve the question, and the narrative does not dwell on it. The exchange happens and the account moves on.

The silence is consistent with the way the narrative handles the man's endowment. He was given the means to all things, and communication with a people who cannot be understood falls within that description without needing to be specified.

**Why the Verse Notes the Difficulty**

The detail is not incidental. A people who can barely make themselves understood are a people who cannot easily ask for help, and the following verse records them making a request anyway.

The effort implied is significant. They identify a threat, propose a solution, and offer payment, all across a communication barrier. The verse establishes how much that cost them before recording what they said.

That framing shapes the reader's response to the request. It is not the demand of a people with options; it is the appeal of a people who have managed, with difficulty, to put their case.

Noting the difficulty also sets the reader's expectation. A petition delivered with effort will be short, and the verse that follows delivers exactly that.

**Their Position Relative to the Threat**

The verse places them in front of the mountains — on the near side, between the traveller and whatever lies beyond. The commentators describe them as dwelling on the southern side of the ranges.

The position explains their exposure. They are not behind the barrier; they are in front of it, on the side from which the threat approaches, and the gap between the mountains is the route by which it reaches them.

The verse therefore sets up the construction that follows with a single preposition. The people are on the wrong side of the only natural defence the land offers.

Their situation is also why they can offer nothing but tribute. A people who cannot defend a mountain gap cannot be expected to build across it, and the request is proportionate to what they have rather than to what they need.

**The Third Arrival and Its Difference**

The first two journeys ended with observations: a people in the west, a people in the east. This one ends with a people who speak.

The difference is the point. The account has covered the earth and found two nations it merely describes; the third is the one that addresses the traveller, and the narrative expands to accommodate the exchange.

The verse is therefore the hinge of the whole account. Everything before it is survey; everything after it is a request answered.

The difference is that this arrival produces a request. The first two encounters ended in observation or decision; this one ends in a petition, and the verse prepares for it.

The Qur'an describes speech itself as a gift rather than an attainment. Sūrat al-Raḥmān lists the teaching of speech among the first things given to the human being, and Sūrat al-Naml records Solomon given the language of birds as a specific endowment (27:16).

The parallel identifies comprehension as something conferred. A people who can barely understand speech are not deficient in a moral sense; they are at the edge of a capacity that varies between them.

Read against that, the verse's description is a fact about circumstance, and the account uses it to measure the effort behind the request that follows.

**A People Who Could Barely Speak**

The verse adds one detail about the people at the gap: they could hardly understand speech. The wording describes a difficulty of communication rather than an inability.

The detail explains the shape of the request that follows. People who struggle to make themselves understood ask in the simplest terms available, and the petition recorded in the next verse is exactly that short.

The verse therefore prepares the exchange by describing both parties' positions. One side can barely articulate its need; the other has been given the means to all things, and the account lets the mismatch speak for itself.

The wording stops short of describing them as unintelligible. They can communicate, but not easily, and the distinction is what makes the exchange that follows plausible.

The verse opens with geography rather than with the people who live there. Two mountains and the pass between them are named first, and the people are introduced only afterwards.

The order is necessary. The gap explains the vulnerability, and the vulnerability explains the request, so the account cannot state the request intelligibly until the terrain has been given.

The same method governs the sūrah's other descriptions. The garden is described before the boast, and the cave before the sleep. In each case the setting is established first because the action only makes sense inside it.

The precedence of terrain over people is unusual in this account, and it is justified here because the terrain is the reason the people need anything at all.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:94

> They pleaded, “O Ⱬul-Qarnain! Surely Gog and Magog are spreading corruption throughout the land. Should we pay you tribute, provided that you build a wall between us and them?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Request That Opens With a Complaint**

The people begin with what is happening to them: Gog and Magog are spreading corruption in the land. The complaint comes before the request, and the request is framed as a question.

The order is significant. They do not open with an offer of payment or an appeal to the traveller's power. They state the problem, and only then ask whether a solution can be bought.

The form of the question is also careful. *Should we pay you tribute* is a proposal, not a demand, and it leaves the traveller free to refuse the payment as well as the work.

Opening with the complaint establishes urgency before the proposal is made, and the order is what makes the petition read as a necessity rather than a preference.

**Yaʾjūj and Maʾjūj: Who They Are**

The names are Yaʾjūj and Maʾjūj, rendered in English as Gog and Magog. The commentators trace them to the line of Japheth, son of Noah, and the same names appear in the Hebrew scriptures and the New Testament as forces of destruction — Gog of the land of Magog in Ezekiel, and the nations gathered at the end in Revelation.

Some commentators derive the names from Arabic roots: one from a word meaning to kindle a fire, the other from the word for waves. Both images suggest destructive force rather than a specific nation.

Speculation about their identity has been extensive — the Turks and the Daylamites have both been proposed — and the proposals rest on the reputation these names carried rather than on anything the text supplies. The Qur'an does not locate them, count them, or describe them.

**"Spreading Corruption in the Land"**

The term used is the Qur'an's standard word for corruption on earth, which covers both moral ruin and violent wrongdoing. The commentators explain it concretely: the reports describe Gog and Magog devouring people and consuming the resources of the land, and the people as having suffered slaughter at their hands.

The wording is general enough to cover all of it. The verse does not itemise the harm; it names the condition, and the request that follows measures how bad the condition is.

The term also connects the account to the rest of the Qur'an. Corruption in the land is a category with established meaning, and using it places these people inside a pattern the reader already recognises.

**The Offer of Tribute**

The proposal is a payment in exchange for a barrier. The people offer to assign a tribute, and the work they ask for is specified: a barrier between them and the threat.

The offer is honest about their position. They have something to give and they know it is not enough on its own, which is why the proposal is put as a question rather than a transaction.

The verse records the offer without endorsing it, and the reply in the following verse declines the payment while accepting the work. The account is careful to let the offer stand as the people made it.

The tribute is offered rather than demanded, and the distinction matters for what follows, since a gift freely offered can be freely declined without offence.

**A Future Unleashing**

The Qur'an mentions Gog and Magog elsewhere in an eschatological context. Sūrat al-Anbiyāʾ describes the barrier holding *“until ˹after˺ Gog and Magog have broken loose ˹from the barrier˺, swarming down from every hill, ushering in the True Promise”* (21:96–97).

Some commentators read the corruption described in this verse as belonging to that later period rather than to the present of the narrative. The verse itself does not decide, and the request is made in the present tense.

The connection matters for the account's ending. The barrier built here is not permanent, and the sūrah says so in the words of the man who builds it.

**What the Request Reveals About the People**

The verse describes people who have identified a threat they cannot meet, proposed a solution they cannot build, and offered what they have toward it. Nothing in the request claims a right.

The address is also telling. They call him by his title rather than by any claim of kinship or alliance, which means the appeal rests entirely on his capacity and their need.

That is the position the account wants to establish before the reply. A people with nothing but a problem and a proposal, addressing a ruler who has been given the earth.

The contrast is complete in a single verse. Everything the people lack — strength, materials, the ability even to be understood easily — the ruler has been given, and the account arranges the encounter so that the mismatch is visible before anyone speaks.

The mismatch is also what makes the reply significant. A refusal of payment from someone who needed it would be self-denial; from someone who does not, it is simply an accurate statement of where the resources are.

**The Same Threat in Sūrat al-Anbiyāʾ**

The parallel places Gog and Magog in the events preceding the Hour: *“until ˹after˺ Gog and Magog have broken loose ˹from the barrier˺, swarming down from every hill, ushering in the True Promise”* (21:96–97).

Sūrat al-Anbiyāʾ supplies what this verse's request implies — that the barrier is a delay rather than a solution, and that its end is fixed.

Read together, the two passages frame the construction that follows. The people are asking for protection that will hold until a date none of them knows.

The forward reference is what keeps the barrier from reading as a permanent solution. The same account that describes the construction also describes the day the barrier fails.

**Corruption in the Land as the Reason**

The people do not describe a battle or an army. They describe corruption — spreading disorder in the land — as the reason they need a barrier.

The wording is broader than warfare and matches the Qur'an's use of the same term for general ruin and violence. What is feared is not a single raid but a condition of the region.

That framing also determines what the solution must be. A threat of disorder requires a permanent obstruction rather than a military response, and the barrier built in the following verses is exactly that.

Naming the reason also limits the request. A people facing raiders ask for a defence; a people facing general disorder ask for an obstruction, and the barrier answers the second.

**A Request Phrased as an Offer**

The people do not simply ask for help. They offer tribute in exchange for a barrier, and the verse records the terms of their proposal as well as its object.

The phrasing is significant. A people who can barely make themselves understood have managed a complete proposition, which shows the seriousness of what they are facing.

The offer also sets up the refusal that follows. The ruler declines the payment and asks for labour instead, and the exchange only makes sense because the verse has recorded what was offered first.

The offer is complete in itself, and its completeness is what makes the refusal in the next verse meaningful rather than arbitrary.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:95

> He responded, “What my Lord has provided for me is far better. But assist me with resources, and I will build a barrier between you and them

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Payment Refused**

The reply declines the tribute in its first clause. What my Lord has established me in is better. The ruler does not negotiate the amount or defer the decision; he states that he does not need what they are offering.

The wording locates the reason in what he has been given rather than in his own generosity. The refusal is not a display of magnanimity but a statement of fact about his endowment.

The commentators read the clause as establishing that God had given him such wealth and such an expanse of land that the people's offer was irrelevant to him, and that he undertook the work for the sake of righteousness rather than for payment.

Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr both note the parallel with Solomon's answer to the gifts sent by the Queen of Sheba: *“Do you offer me wealth? What Allah has granted me is far greater than what He has granted you. No! It is you who rejoice in ˹receiving˺ gifts”* (27:36).

The two replies share a structure. A ruler offered payment compares what is offered with what he has been given, and finds the offer smaller. Neither reply disparages the giver; both relocate the comparison.

The parallel identifies a category. A ruler who has been established by God does not take payment for protecting people, and the Qur'an records two such rulers saying so in nearly the same terms.

**Asking for Labour Rather Than Money**

The second clause asks for something: help me with strength. The commentators explain it as a request for able-bodied men, tools, and materials.

The distinction between the two clauses is precise. Money is refused; labour is requested. The ruler will not take their wealth but will use their hands, which converts the transaction into a joint effort.

The request also acknowledges a limit. A man given the means to all things still asks the people for workers, which means the barrier is described as a shared work rather than a gift delivered from above.

The arrangement has a further effect. A people who contribute labour to their own protection are participants in it rather than recipients, and the verse gives them that standing while refusing their money.

The distinction is not a technicality. A barrier paid for is a service delivered; a barrier built together is a common work, and the account chooses the second without commenting on the choice.

**A Barrier, Not a Wall**

The word used here differs from the one the people used. They asked for a *sadd*, a wall or dam; he promises a *radm*, which denotes something built up by heaping material — a rampart or a filled barrier.

The shift is small and meaningful. A *radm* is a mass rather than a structure, and the description of its construction in the following verses — iron blocks filled between the mountain sides and copper poured over them — matches the word.

The change also signals that he is building something larger than what was requested. The people asked for a wall; they are offered a filled rampart spanning a mountain gap.

**The Contrast With the Owner of the Gardens**
