The structure is a conditional with an unexpected result. The reader expects the image to establish the vastness of the Words; the verse establishes instead the finitude of the ocean.

That reversal is the verse's argument. The point is not that the Words are large but that the sea — the largest thing the hearer can imagine — is limited, and the comparison therefore fails in the direction that matters.

The verse therefore uses an image of abundance to prove scarcity. The ocean is the standard of inexhaustibility in ordinary speech, and the verse demotes it.

**What the Words Are**

The commentators differ over the referent. Some take the Words as God's knowledge and wisdom; others take them as the meanings and benefits contained in revelation; others as God's capacity to create and to command whatever He wills.

The Qur'an describes creation as effected by a Word — when God decrees a thing He says to it "Be", and it is. On that reading the Words are the acts of creation themselves, which are without number.

Others connect the phrase to the passage's context. The preceding verses discuss reward and punishment, and some commentators take the Words here as the promises and threats of the Hereafter, or as what God will say to the people of the Garden.

The verse does not choose among these readings, and the image works with any of them. What is asserted is not the content of the Words but the fact that they cannot be written out.

**The Ocean Exhausted**

The verse states the result plainly: the ocean would be exhausted before the Words were finished. The wording puts the two exhaustions in sequence and names which comes first.

The sequencing is what makes the image work. A comparison of two infinite quantities proves nothing; a comparison in which one runs out and the other does not establishes a difference in kind.

The verse therefore claims something stronger than magnitude. It claims that the Words are not the sort of thing that can be measured against a quantity, however large.

That is the same claim the chapter made about knowledge in a different register. When Mūsā was told that God's knowledge had not decreased by what the sparrow took from the sea, the image was the same and the point was identical.

**Refilled With Its Equal**

The final clause extends the test. Even if the ocean were refilled with another ocean like it, the Words would still not be exhausted.

The addition is not decoration. It removes the obvious objection — that the ocean simply needs to be larger — by granting the objection and showing that it does not help.

Doubling a finite quantity leaves it finite. The verse therefore anticipates the reader's attempt to rescue the comparison and closes it in advance.

The clause also fixes the logic of the verse. The Words are not merely more than the sea; they are of a different order, and no number of seas reaches them.

**The Same Image in Sūrat Luqmān**

The Qur'an uses the same image with more detail: *“If all the trees on earth were pens and the ocean ˹were ink˺, refilled by seven other oceans, the Words of Allah would not be exhausted. Surely Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.”* (31:27)

The parallel extends the comparison in both directions. Trees become pens as well as oceans becoming ink, and the refilling is multiplied to seven seas rather than one.

The conclusion is identical, and the closing statement of that verse supplies what this one leaves implicit: the inexhaustibility belongs to God's might and wisdom rather than to a quantity of words.

Read together, the two verses establish the claim twice, with the second giving the reason the first asserts.

**The Verse's Place in the Chapter's Closing**

The verse stands between the account of the two accommodations and the chapter's final statement. It interrupts the sequence with a claim about God's Words rather than about human outcomes.

The interruption is purposeful. The chapter has described what people do and what follows from it; this verse reminds the reader that what God says is not bounded by anything the chapter has described.

The placement therefore reorders the reader's sense of scale immediately before the closing verse. After a chapter of gardens, caves, journeys, and barriers, the sūrah states that none of it approaches the Words, and then ends by reducing the Prophet to a man like his hearers.

The two verses together set the chapter's final proportions: God's Words are inexhaustible, and the one who carries them is human.

**An Image That Works Whatever the Words Are**

The verse does not require the reader to settle the question of what the Words are. Ocean, ink, and exhaustion do their work whether the Words are taken as knowledge, as revelation, or as the acts of creation.

That indeterminacy is deliberate. A claim about magnitude would depend on the referent; a claim about kind does not, and the verse makes the second claim.

The reader is therefore asked to accept something that does not depend on resolving a difficulty, which is how the chapter handles its other unresolved questions.

**The Verse Reduces the Reader's Scale**

After a chapter of vast journeys and great constructions, the verse declares the ocean insufficient. The largest thing the hearer can picture is used up before the Words are written down.

The reduction is the verse's function at this point in the sūrah. It arrives immediately before the closing statement about the Prophet's humanity, and the two verses together set the proportions of everything the chapter has described.

The effect is to place the reader correctly. The gardens, the cave, and the barrier are real, and none of them approaches what the verse describes.

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:110

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I am only a man like you, ˹but˺ it has been revealed to me that your God is only One God. So whoever hopes for the meeting with their Lord, let them do good deeds and associate none in the worship of their Lord.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter Ends With a Denial of Exception**

The sūrah's last verse opens with the Prophet describing himself as a man like his hearers. After a chapter containing a cave, a journey to the ends of the earth, and a barrier of iron and copper, the closing statement is a claim of ordinariness.

The placement is deliberate. The verse could have ended with a promise or a threat; it ends by removing any special standing from the person delivering the message.

The denial is not modesty presented as virtue. It is a claim about the structure of revelation — the messenger shares the hearers' nature, and what distinguishes him is not his nature but what has been revealed to him.

The verse therefore answers an objection the Qur'an records repeatedly. People doubted the prophets precisely because they were human, and this verse accepts the premise and refuses the conclusion.

**The Same Claim Made by Other Messengers**

The Qur'an puts the same statement in the mouths of earlier messengers. In Sūrat Ibrāhīm the messengers say to their people: *“We are ˹indeed˺ only humans like you, but Allah favours whoever He chooses of His servants.”* (14:11)

Sūrat al-Isrāʾ records the same reply to a demand for miracles: *“Glory be to my Lord! Am I not only a human messenger?”* (17:93)

The consistency shows that the claim is not specific to this Prophet. It is the standard answer of the messengers to the demand that they be something other than human, and this verse places the final revelation inside that line.

The verse therefore closes the chapter by placing it in a sequence. What is being said at the end of Sūrat al-Kahf has been said by every messenger before it.

**What Has Been Revealed**

The second clause states the content of the revelation in the shortest possible form: your God is one God.

The wording is exact. It does not say God is one in the abstract; it says your God — the one you already address — is one. The claim is about the identity of the object of worship rather than about a metaphysical proposition.

That framing answers the chapter's opening concern. The sūrah began by warning those who said God has taken a son, and it ends by stating the opposite claim in the plainest terms available.

The verse therefore brackets the chapter. The error named in the first verses is answered in the last, and the answer is given as the entire content of the revelation rather than as one doctrine among many.

**Hope for the Meeting**

The third clause introduces the condition: whoever hopes for the meeting with his Lord. The verse addresses hope rather than fear, and the object of the hope is the meeting itself.

The choice is significant in a chapter that has described the meeting's consequences in detail. The Fire was displayed, the deeds were weighed, and the two accommodations were named; the closing verse addresses the person who looks forward to it.

The wording also ties back to the verse that described the greatest losers. Those people rejected the meeting; this verse addresses whoever hopes for it, and the two positions are the chapter's final pair.

The verse therefore ends by restating the division it established three verses earlier, but from the side of hope rather than loss.

**Two Requirements Stated**

The final clause gives two instructions: do righteous deeds, and associate no partner with the Lord in worship.

The two correspond to the two conditions the earlier verses named for reward. Belief and righteous action were joined throughout; here the belief is specified as the exclusion of partners, and the action is stated as righteous deeds.

The order is also informative. The deed comes first and the exclusion of partners second, which places the practice before the doctrinal clarification rather than after it.

The verse therefore ends with instruction rather than description. After a chapter of narrative, the last words are directives, and they are addressed to whoever hopes.

**The Warning Against the Lesser Form**

The commentators note that believers are by definition free of open association of partners, and they cite a report in which the Prophet was asked about the lesser form of it and answered that it is ostentation in religious acts.

The citation extends the verse's final clause beyond its obvious application. The exclusion of partners is not only a matter of not worshipping idols; it includes not directing an act of worship toward the observation of other people.

The connection is consistent with the chapter's concern. The garden owner wanted his estate seen, and the sūrah treats that wanting as the beginning of his failure. The final verse excludes the same impulse from worship.

The verse therefore closes the chapter by naming the subtlest form of the error the parable illustrated, and it leaves the identification of that form to the reader.

**A Closing Verse That Removes the Messenger From the Claim**

The verse's final instruction is about worship, and the one delivering it has just described himself as a man like his hearers. Nothing in the verse asks for anything on his own account.

That structure answers the objection the Qur'an records throughout. The messengers were doubted because they were human, and this verse accepts the humanity and directs the worship elsewhere.

The chapter therefore ends without asking anything of the reader except what it asked at the beginning: attention to a revelation delivered by a man, and worship directed to the One who sent it.

**The Chapter's Two Ends Match**

The sūrah opened with a warning to those who claimed God had taken a son, and it closes with the statement that God is one and that no partner is to be associated with Him in worship.

The two verses are the same claim stated at opposite ends of a long chapter. Everything between them — the cave, the gardens, the journeys, the barrier, the Day — stands inside that frame.

The verse therefore completes the chapter by returning it to its starting point, and it leaves the reader with the instruction rather than with the narrative.
